#10522. Power and cooling limits force AI clusters with 100,000+ accelerators to use multiple data centers

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

And software gains importance for such distributed data centers.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The rapid growth of AI clusters is pushing computing infrastructure beyond the physical and power limitations of individual data centers, which creates demand for new networking architectures capable of connecting compute resources across multiple facilities and even geographical regions. At Supercomputing 2026 (SC26), researchers from The Ohio State University discussed the evolution of high-performance networking technologies. At the conference, they explained how efficient distributed computing at such a scale requires considerably more than faster network connections, highlighting the importance of scale across domains to stretch workloads across different data centers in separate geographical regions.

Modern AI clusters typically accommodate more than 100,000 AI accelerators and potentially millions of CPU endpoints, placing tremendous demands on network bandwidth, latency, and congestion management. Meanwhile, an accelerator's computational performance continues to increase faster than communication capabilities, which means that faster AI GPUs do not necessarily deliver proportional improvements in application performance. As a result, the efficiency of network infrastructure determines how effectively large-scale AI systems can use their computational resources.

Traditionally, high-performance computing systems have relied on two major interconnect domains: scale-up and scale-out. Scale-up networks connect processors and accelerators within tightly integrated systems using technologies like AMD's Infinity Fabric, Intel's UPI, Nvidia's NVLink, or emerging alternatives such as UALink. Scale-out networks, which typically rely on Ethernet, InfiniBand, HPE Slingshot, or, more recently, Ultra Ethernet, connect individual servers or rack-scale systems into larger clusters. These interconnects have different requirements for bandwidth, latency, and scalability, which is why they serve different purposes.

However, the massive size and power consumption of modern AI clusters create demand for a third interconnect domain, known as scale-across. Unlike scale-out networks, which typically connect systems within a single data center, scale-across architectures extend high-performance connectivity between different buildings, campuses, or even regions.

Such an approach enables operators to combine computational and other resources from multiple locations to achieve a certain level of performance rather than to bring an excessive number of accelerators to a single data center, which may be hard, or even impossible, due to power availability, cooling requirements, or physical space constraints.

The transition toward scale-across architectures has already begun. Google introduced its Virgo network architecture earlier this year to support AI workloads that exceed the space and power capacity of individual data centers. The company intends to treat an entire campus of computing facilities as a single system, albeit with specialized networks for communication between accelerators, servers, and data centers. Cisco and Marvell are also developing technologies for scale-across deployments, including high-capacity routers, switching chips, and optical interconnects designed to connect geographically distributed clusters.

Extending AI infrastructure beyond individual facilities introduces substantial technical challenges. While modern scale-up networks offer extremely high bandwidth and low latency over relatively short distances, communication between data centers must contend with the physical limitations of long-distance connections. Even optical networks cannot eliminate propagation delays, whereas congestion, packet loss, and synchronization overhead can further reduce effective performance. Consequently, connecting two large GPU clusters with high-speed links does not necessarily make them behave like one tightly integrated computing system.

These challenges are particularly important for AI training, where accelerators frequently exchange model parameters, gradients, and intermediate results. Large-scale workloads require efficient collective communications, as delays that affect individual accelerators dramatically reduce the performance of the entire cluster. Therefore, the efficiency of distributed computing depends not only on raw network bandwidth, but also on network topology, congestion management, communication protocols, and software implementations.

To address these limitations, network adapters and switches are becoming more sophisticated than ever. Modern network interface controllers can perform Remote Direct Memory Access (RDMA) operations without continuous CPU involvement, whereas technologies such as HPE Slingshot hardware collectives and Nvidia SHARP can offload certain communication operations to the network infrastructure itself. SmartNICs and data processing units further reduce the burden on host processors as they handle selected network and infrastructure functions in dedicated hardware. Such capabilities can improve efficiency of distributed applications without necessarily requiring faster physical interconnects.

Interestingly, software plays an equally important role in the performance of large-scale AI infrastructure, as communication frameworks like libfabric, MPI, or UCX determine how applications exchange data and use the capabilities of modern network adapters. Also, GPU-aware communication and technologies like GPUDirect RDMA can eliminate unnecessary transfers through host memory, while optimized collective operations can reduce communication overhead across large numbers of accelerators, according to the researchers.

The growing complexity of AI infrastructure also changes how operators should evaluate network performance. Although the industry continues to introduce faster interconnect technologies, including versions of GbE with nominal bandwidths of 800 Gb/s and 1.6 Tb/s, peak transfer rates alone do not fully describe the capabilities of a given system because the performance of actual applications depends on multiple factors, including how effectively network hardware and software cooperate to transfer data, prevent congestion, and synchronize computations across thousands of processors.

Ultimately, the emergence of scale-across interconnection architectures reflects a fundamental change in the design of AI infrastructure. As the computational capabilities of individual accelerators increase and AI clusters expand beyond individual facilities, efficient communication becomes just as important as processor performance. As a result, the next generation of AI systems will depend not only on faster AI accelerators or CPUs, but also on sophisticated network adapters, switches, and, perhaps more importantly, software that can unite geographically distributed computational resources without excessive communication overhead.

Anton Shilov is a contributing writer at Tom’s Hardware. Over the past couple of decades, he has covered everything from CPUs and GPUs to supercomputers and from modern process technologies and latest fab tools to high-tech industry trends.

#10520. How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

AI agents now read email, book travel and move money for the people who use them. Every tool call also pulls in text that someone else wrote, and whoever controls that text can try to steer the agent. OWASP calls this agent goal hijack and ranks it first in its Top 10 for Agentic Applications (2026).

In September we announced Agent Anomaly Detection in Private Preview on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform. It reads your agents' traces and logs asynchronously, without adding latency, and flags sessions where an agent acts outside its intended boundaries. We tested this feature against benchmarks for this top risk of agent goal hijack.

Security tools tend to look good on tests their own team wrote, so we ran five non-Google-written, public agent-security benchmarks through Agent Anomaly Detection without changing anything. In four of them, the harmful instruction hides in data that a tool returns. In the fifth, the user asks for harm directly.

The model itself didn't follow about half of the harmful instructions it read. It refused three in four direct requests for harm, and ignored or failed at about 30% of the instructions hidden in tool output. Of the ones that were executed, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% for post-execution review. It raised false alarms on 2% to 16% of benign sessions, depending on the tools the agent uses. Poisoned memory is the clear gap: when instructions planted in the agent’s long-term memory made it act, Agent Anomaly Detection caught 4 of 18.

We ran each benchmark case through an agent built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) using gemini-2.5-flash, on the benchmark's simulated apps. The agent sent its traces and logs to Agent Anomaly Detection, as a customer's agent would. Then the benchmark's own Python check read the final state of the apps, such as whether money left the account, and labeled the session. Deterministic code decides the label, not a model, so an injected instruction can't mislead the grader, and Agent Anomaly Detection cannot cheat the benchmark. Each agent first ran at least 300 benign sessions, because Agent Anomaly Detection learns how each agent usually behaves.

We also recorded whether a tool returned the harmful instruction to the agent. In many cases, the agent never opens the email or file that holds it. So each harmful session ends one of three ways:

In AgentHarm, the user sends the request, so the agent always reads it.

The five benchmarks are AgentDojo (NeurIPS 2024), InjecAgent (ACL 2024 Findings), Agent Security Bench (ASB, ICLR 2025), AgentDyn (2026) and AgentHarm (ICLR 2025). ASB plants the instruction in the agent's long-term memory. In all, Agent Anomaly Detection scored 1,774 sessions.

Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% of the harmful instructions that the agent carried out, and raised a few false alarms on ordinary office work. It's strongest on goal hijack and tool misuse, and weakest on poisoned memory.

Each finding carries a threat category, and four of Agent Anomaly Detection’s five categories align with the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. We graded each OWASP category on what this study showed, not on what Agent Anomaly Detection is designed to catch.

Public benchmarks concentrate on goal hijack and tool misuse, so our evidence is strongest there. We did not find public benchmarks with labeled agent traces for cascading failures or for identity and privilege abuse. Shared benchmarks for those categories would help the whole field.

By benchmark, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 85% of the carried-out harmful instructions on AgentDojo, 87% on InjecAgent, 99% on AgentDyn and 81% on AgentHarm. Over all harmful sessions, including the ones that the agent never read or resisted, it flagged 45% to 62%. In the 141 sessions where the agent never read the instruction, nothing happened, and it flagged just 1.

Results held across the apps. In 8 of the 11 apps, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged at least 95% of the carried-out harmful instructions. The lowest rate was in AgentDojo's Slack app, at 63%. Many of those instructions ask for one small action, such as a page visit, which looks like normal browsing.

Agent Anomaly Detection also flags many requests that the agent refuses. In AgentHarm, the agent refused most requests and carried out 108 of 412, and Agent Anomaly Detection still flagged 55% of the refused sessions, because the request sits in the user's own message, which it reads. When the agent didn't follow an instruction hidden in tool output, it flagged only 11% to 25%, because the instruction sits deep inside a tool result.

False alarms were rare on ordinary office work: on the three injection benchmarks, whose benign sessions are office tasks, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 2% to 4% of benign sessions. On AgentHarm it flagged 16%. Those benign tasks are twins of the harmful ones, so they post to social media, run terminal commands and download files. High-impact tools in normal use look more like misuse.

Agent Anomaly Detection did less well in five areas. It flagged only 11% to 25% of the instructions that the agent resisted, because a resisted instruction changes little in how the agent behaves. It missed more small actions, like single page visits, because they look like normal work. And it raised more false alarms for agents whose everyday work uses high-impact tools. If your agent is one of them, expect more findings at first, and triage them by severity and threat category.

The other two areas are new agents and poisoned memory. Some Agent Anomaly Detection methods need a few hundred sessions of history: on AgentDyn, it flagged 33% of carried-out harmful instructions when each agent had about 220 benign sessions, and 99% with 360. And it caught only 4 of the 18 sessions where poisoned memory made the agent act.

Protect your agents with defense in depth. Use controls that stop harmful actions before they run, and Agent Anomaly Detection to find what gets through.

We used one agent model and simulated apps, and the benchmarks' benign tasks are likely simpler than your production traffic, so your false-alarm rate can differ. Agent Anomaly Detection returned no result for about 6% of the sessions we sent; counting those as misses lowers the overall rates by 1 to 2 points.

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview. To request access, read the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.

#10513. MSI EdgeMesa N AI+ review: RTX Spark power comes to Windows on Arm desktops, but there are trade-offs

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get started with free access to reviews, badges and discussions.

Dive into our proprietary testing data and compare hardware with detailed benchmarks.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

MSI's EdgeMesa N AI+ mini PC brings Nvidia RTX Spark power to a Windows on Arm desktop. It offers broad app compatibility and the same strong local AI performance we've come to expect from Nvidia's GB10 SoC, but it's still very expensive and less expandable than DGX Spark systems.

Solid local AI performance for LLMs and image generation, as expected

Familiar Windows 11 user experience and app compatibility

Could use more peripheral I/O and smarter port placement

Loses the DGX Spark's clustering capability

Getting the best gaming performance requires patience

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Nvidia’s RTX Spark platform, powered by the RTX Spark N1X SoC, passed its most difficult test yet last month at the heart of Windows on Arm laptops. Now we get to see how that chip serves as a Windows on Arm foundation for compact desktops.

We’ve got our hands on the MSI EdgeMesa N AI+, a $7,500 RTX Spark mini PC that gives Windows-native creators and local AI trailblazers access to the same powerful 6144-core Blackwell GPU and 20-core Arm CPU that we recently put to the test in the Surface Laptop Ultra, along with a 128GB pool of unified memory and 4TB of storage in our tested config.

We’ve been testing Nvidia GB10-powered desktop systems for almost a year now in the form of the DGX Spark and friends, and the EdgeMesa N AI+’s design is typical of the breed. It’s wrapped in a slightly hollow-feeling matte aluminum shell with shiny diamond-cut edges and a white plastic back plate.

The design and build quality of this system is a tad underwhelming for the price. It feels nice enough, but It’s hardly a precision-milled statement piece for your desk like a Mac Mini or Mac Studio.

The EdgeMesa draws cool air in through its perforated front panel, which then passes through a dual-fan heatsink with a vapor-chamber base and exhausts through the back panel. The rounded rectangle on the top half of the front panel might look like an optical drive tray, but it’s a purely cosmetic accent.

Around back, the EdgeMesa comes equipped with a 10 Gigabit Ethernet jack, an HDMI 2.1 port, and three USB Type-C 20 Gbps ports with DisplayPort Alt Mode support. A fourth USB-C port is dedicated to the system’s 240 W USB-PD power adapter. The EdgeMesa also has Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 5.4 radios inside.

Even with a wireless keyboard and mouse, three 20Gbps USB-C ports feel a bit skimpy as the sole expansion options on a $7,499 PC that’s positioned as a do-it-all desktop for content creation and gaming. If you need to connect multiple monitors to an RTX Spark via USB-C, those scant options for peripheral connectivity shrink even further.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

For comparison, a similarly priced Mac Studio gives you four 80Gbps Thunderbolt 5 ports around back and two more USB Type-C ports, plus an SD card reader, up front. The Mac even provides a headphone jack, while the EdgeMesa requires you to plug in a USB headset or DAC or pair Bluetooth earbuds for audio output.

Even the Mac Mini gives you three Thunderbolt ports out back and two USB 3 10Gbps ports out front.

In use, the tightly spaced, rear-mounted USB-C ports make for an annoying time when doing frequent unplugging and replugging of devices like card readers and external SSDs that are typical in creative workflows. Even one front-panel USB-C port would have made a huge difference in the ease of use of this design.

The EdgeMesa also drops the handy ConnectX RDMA NIC that lets you cluster DGX Spark systems together to run larger AI models. That omission raises a weighty question: is Windows support and application compatibility important enough to trade away any chance of expanding your local AI foundation in the future?

Having tested DGX Sparks for some time now, I’m not sure that’s a wise trade-off, given that most anybody reading this already has a capable Windows PC or Mac that can link up with a DGX Spark for many local AI compute jobs using the Nvidia Sync app.

DGX Spark prices are rising, but you can get MSI's own EdgeXpert with 128GB of memory and a 4TB SSD on sale for $8,099 right now (or $8,499 at full price). Even a $1000 difference in price for that ConnectX NIC in this rarefied air of PC pricing would give me pause if I'm seriously considering how to add local AI capacity to my home.

If you have no inclination whatsoever to learn Linux or networking concepts and just want a no-questions-asked, AI-capable Windows mini PC that can serve as your primary desktop, an RTX Spark system like this may be the way to go. But you'll want to be really sure of your decision before dropping this much cash.

20-core Arm CPU (10 X925 performance cores, 10 A725 efficiency cores)

6144-core (48 SM) Nvidia Blackwell integrated GPU

128GB LPDDR5X, 273 GB/s shared memory bandwidth

10 Gigabit Ethernet, Wi-Fi 7, Bluetooth 5.4

The performance of the Nvidia GB10 SoC's CPU and GPU resources, whether under DGX OS or Windows, is well-trodden ground by this point. But for a fresh point of comparison, we grabbed AMD’s Ryzen AI Halo, swapped in a spare SSD, and installed Windows 11 Pro, as the Ryzen AI Halo is the only other Windows-ready platform with a large unified memory pool for AI adventurers.

In the Geekbench 7 CPU benchmark under Windows, the Ryzen AI Halo delivers a slightly higher single-threaded score than the MSI EdgeMesa’s RTX Spark Superchip, but its multithreaded score trails the Nvidia part. Using Linux, we would expect both scores to be higher for the Ryzen CPU. But the broader point remains the same: you can expect similar performance from both of these CPUs under Windows.

We’ve left Apple’s Mac Studio with M4 Max in these charts just to emphasize the large performance lead Apple Silicon holds in this benchmark, even with older chips. The current M5 Max would likely open an even larger lead.

In the AgentPerf-Local AI inference benchmark, the MSI EdgeMesa delivered performance practically identical to Microsoft’s Surface Laptop Ultra when running the same Qwen 3.8 27B model with DFlash2 speculative decoding. Its desktop form factor doesn’t seem to give it any extra performance headroom, at least for this LLM serving configuration. Other workloads or models could reveal a difference.

We already tested the RTX Spark against the Ryzen AI Halo in AgentPerf-Local in our Surface Laptop Ultra review, and AMD’s platform ended up way behind GB10 in those tests. And in this new round of testing, AMD's LLM performance is worse under Windows with the Vulkan backend than in Linux with ROCm, taking a whopping four times as long as the EdgeMesa to complete the benchmark.

Strix Halo might have been an appealing local inference option when it was the only open and relatively hackable unified memory platform around, but if you’re serious about local AI performance (and especially if tokens equal dollars for you), a GB10 system is the superior choice in late 2026.

Gaming on RTX Spark continues to be a bit of a weird time, and the MSI EdgeMesa N doesn’t change that experience, even with a more generous power and thermal budget than the Surface Laptop Ultra I've already tested.

This platform just doesn’t respond to tuning as we might expect from our experience with traditional gaming PCs and laptops. That weirdness stems, in part, from the imbalance between memory bandwidth and compute resources on GB10.

The 48 SMs of the RTX Spark N1X GPU have to share just 273 GB/s of LPDDR5X bandwidth with the CPU. Compare that with the 672 GB/s of dedicated bandwidth the similarly-specced RTX 5070 enjoys from its 12 GB of GDDR7.

Architectural decisions aside, the RTX Spark platform's gaming performance isn't helped by the fact that full GPU utilization is seemingly hampered by the overhead of Prism x86 emulation in most games. Few (if any) major titles have Arm ports, so this is just a fact of life for the foreseeable future if you buy one of these systems.

Let's see how this all plays out in practice for the RTX Spark versus the Radeon 8060S on board AMD's Strix Halo.

In the 3DMark Steel Nomad raster benchmark, the RTX Spark holds a wide lead over the Ryzen AI Halo, delivering a roughly 56% higher score. That result would suggest a knockout in games for Nvidia’s platform.

But Steel Nomad isn’t representative of real-world gaming performance on the RTX Spark. If we fire up Control Resonant on both platforms and choose the High raster preset at 1440p with Performance upscaling (a 1280x720 input resolution), the RTX Spark and Strix Halo end up a lot closer.

Notably, though, the RTX Spark desktop delivers considerably worse 1% low FPS performance than the Ryzen AI Halo, and this does manifest as visible stutter and hitching. So it's not a clear win for the Nvidia platform.

AMD fans might take heart in the fact that Strix Halo can get this close to the RTX Spark on this raster gaming baseline, but the Radeon 8060S doesn’t have nearly as many tricks up its sleeve to further juice performance.

For one, the quality of FSR 4 upscaling at lower input resolutions on RDNA 3.5 is still not up to par with what you can get out of DLSS 4.5. For two, the Radeon 8060S’s RDNA 3.5 CUs don't match up well against the Spark's Blackwell-powered RT chops.

And FSR Frame Generation is still limited to a doubling of a base frame rate, so you can’t smooth out gameplay on a high-refresh-rate monitor nearly as well as you can with DLSS MFG.

So even if their raster baseline frame rates are similar, the Radeon 8060S isn't delivering the same image quality in Control Resonant as an RTX Spark system does with all its DLSS features in play.

RT is the next step for squeezing better image quality out of Control Resonant. Surprisingly, enabling RT Medium only results in a 14% performance hit on the Spark versus 38% on the AI Halo. That might be due in part to the efficiency of DLSS Ray Reconstruction versus the Northlight engine's built-in denoiser, but the RT prowess of Blackwell certainly can't hurt.

A 52.3 FPS average with a 75 ms average PC latency is an OK baseline — at least in the context of an RTX Spark system — for adding MFG to the mix for a smoother experience, especially since Control Resonant seems to link Nvidia Reflex latency reduction to that tech anyway. But dialing in MFG 3X with the default MFG Preset B model still results in a PC latency of 96 ms on average, which is too high to be enjoyable, even if we give the Spark some slack.

I found that the new MFG Preset C, available through the Nvidia App, significantly lowered latency versus the more compute-intensive Preset B. While input latency with Preset C is still high at about 84 ms, we have to take every bit of help we can get here, and the resulting 145 FPS average is notably smoother than native gameplay on my 160 Hz monitor.

We only had time to dive into this one title in our testing, but when Nvidia says the RTX Spark platform is great for 1440p gaming, our experience suggests that you need to be ready to tweak DLSS upscaling and dial in MFG for more demanding games. And look: that’s fine. These are proven technologies at this point, and they let us get a better experience out of this platform than native rendering would allow.

But all of these data points should go to show that if you're used to the low input latency and predictable performance scaling characteristics of an x86 desktop gaming PC with a discrete GPU, the RTX Spark platform still has a ways to go before it can truly replace such a system.

During our AgentPerf-Local LLM benchmark, the EdgeMesa N AI+ pulls about 140 W from the wall, and under that load, it produces just 38.2 dBA at 18” (46 cm) according to our calibrated SPL meter, well below the 47.4 dBA of the RTX Spark-powered Surface Laptop Ultra and the 48 dBA of the Ryzen AI Halo.

That makes the EdgeMesa an easy system to live with for local AI work, especially if it’s moonlighting as an AI inference server or always-on agent host in shared spaces.

Under gaming workloads, the EdgeMesa draws about 200 W. With more heat to dissipate, the MSI box’s noise output rises to 40.7 dBA, which is still much quieter than the average gaming laptop and will easily be covered by speakers or a closed-back gaming headset.

The Ryzen AI Halo, on the other hand, emits a piercing 46.9 dBA whine while gaming, much closer to what we expect from a gaming laptop. And that’s not because it has that much more heat to dissipate; the AI Halo is pulling about 209 W with Control Resonant running.

Under that same load, the EdgeMesa’s fans have a slightly high-pitched character to them, but it’s much less pronounced than the typical gaming laptop. Mostly, the sound from the system is that of air moving, and it's easy to ignore (so much so that I missed the aural cues I sometimes listen for when a benchmark finishes).

MSI deserves high praise for this system’s thermal design and cooling system. It’s both quiet and pleasant-sounding, and those factors aren’t always a given with mini PCs.

If you’re a hardcore Windows fan who’s been intrigued by Nvidia’s DGX Sparks for local AI, but you've been repelled by the idea of using Linux, RTX Spark desktops like this MSI EdgeMesa N AI+ finally pair your preferred OS with the same 6144-core Nvidia Blackwell GPU, 20-core Arm CPU, and up to 128GB of unified memory that give those systems their local AI grunt.

But that potential comes with tradeoffs that you’ll need to weigh carefully before buying. And at an eye-popping $7,499 for the 128GB EdgeMesa configuration with a 4TB SSD that we tested, those tradeoffs weigh all the more heavily.

The MSI EdgeMesa N AI+ delivers essentially the same performance from the RTX Spark N1X SoC (aka GB10) we’ve come to know and love for about a year now. It’s reasonably fast for local LLM inference, and it offers leading performance with generative AI workflows like images and videos in ComfyUI. Its Arm CPU offers solid enough single-threaded and multi-threaded performance for general computing tasks, and it can game in a pinch. The EdgeMesa is laudably quiet, too — no small feat for a compact PC like this.

But in exchange for Windows support, RTX Spark desktops lose the valuable expandability offered by the ConnectX 7 RDMA NIC that comes standard on DGX Spark boxes. So if you want to try your hand at running a bigger, more intelligent LLM than you can fit in one of these systems’ unified memory pools through clustering, you are forever out of luck if you go down the RTX path.

Even if you can live without the ConnectX NIC, we’re a bit let down by the peripheral connectivity the EdgeMesa N AI+ offers, both for its price and for its ambitions as a do-it-all creator and gaming system.

With just three 20 Gbps USB Type-C ports on the back panel that have to do double duty as display outputs in multi-monitor setups, folks who have to juggle external storage and card readers frequently may find both the number and placement of those ports an obstacle to a smooth workflow. We really would have liked to see more expansion ports on this system overall, or at least one on the front panel.

Nvidia loves to tout the gaming capability of Windows on Arm with RTX Spark systems, but we're still not that impressed by the experience in practice, especially for a system this expensive.

It's nice that you can enjoy PC gaming on these systems at all, but even in a desktop with more power and thermal budget to spare, the gaming performance and responsiveness we saw can only be described as adequate. We were especially surprised by the gulf in 1% low FPS between the RTX Spark and the much cheaper Ryzen AI Halo in Control Resonant, despite the RTX Spark N1X's much stronger GPU on paper.

In general, we find that it takes a lot of careful tweaking and application of DLSS magic to get around this platform's wrinkles for gaming (or a willingness to just let the Nvidia App optimize your game settings). In other words, don't retire your dedicated gaming PC or laptop just yet.

The large memory pools available from RTX Spark systems have uses beyond local AI, of course. Nvidia GPUs are favorites for creative work on Windows, and if you work with massive 3D or game development projects, or you edit lots of high-resolution video, RTX Sparks are the first unified memory systems to pair a powerful Nvidia GPU with this much VRAM for Windows users.

Adobe just updated several of its Creative Suite apps with Arm-native versions, so if you’re trying to blaze the trail of agentic content creation and generation in this modern age, an RTX Spark desktop (or laptop, for that matter) might be intriguing.

But we feel like $7,499 is still a ton of money to spend on a system like this to run Adobe's apps specifically, unless a large unified memory pool is particularly useful for your work today.

All told, we're left scratching our heads a bit about who RTX Spark desktops are really for. RTX Spark laptops meaningfully expanded the capabilities of mobile Windows on Arm systems thanks to the unmatched AI compute resources of their Blackwell GPUs and support for the full Nvidia software stack, along with solid CPU performance and battery life.

But compared to both the existing DGX Spark platform and other mini PCs, RTX Spark desktops like the MSI EdgeMesa N AI+ ask you to give up a lot in exchange for the Nvidia-powered Windows on Arm experience, especially for the high prices they’re likely to command amid the current silicon supply crunch.

If you’re a hardcore Windows-native developer or creator looking to explore the boundaries of what’s possible from local and agentic AI and want the best possible Microsoft-powered foundation for those activities, an RTX Spark desktop might be the right choice for the job, assuming you have the cash.

But if you're just curious, we'd wait for the value proposition of local or agentic AI under Windows to firm up a bit and for the prices of these systems to come down before spending thousands of dollars on a still-fuzzy vision of the future of computing.

As the Senior Analyst, Graphics at Tom's Hardware, Jeff Kampman covers everything that has to do with graphics cards, gaming performance, and more. From integrated graphics processors to discrete graphics cards to the hyperscale installations powering our AI future, if it's got a GPU in it, Jeff is on it.

#10511. Build Live Video Chat With WebRTC: From First Call to Production Debugging

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Adding real-time video to a web app used to mean plugins, proprietary SDKs and a lot of money. Today every modern browser ships with WebRTC, a set of APIs that lets two browsers exchange audio, video and data directly. The catch is that WebRTC gives you building blocks, not a finished feature. This guide walks through those blocks in the order you actually need them, with the exact API calls you need, and then covers the production issues that tutorials usually skip.

A browser-to-browser call has four moving parts:

Start with the local preview. Browsers only allow camera access on HTTPS or localhost, so serve your page accordingly.

1.     Request a stream with navigator.mediaDevices.getUserMedia(), passing video constraints (an ideal width of 1280 and height of 720 is a sensible default) and audio constraints with echoCancellation and noiseSuppression enabled.

2.     Assign the returned MediaStream to the srcObject property of your local video element.

3.     Keep a reference to the stream, because you will add its tracks to the peer connection later.

Set the muted and playsinline attributes on the local video element. Without muted users hear themselves, and without playsinline iOS Safari opens the video full screen.

The peers need a way to find each other. A tiny Node.js server using the ws package is enough for development. Its whole job is to relay messages between the peers in a room:

1.     Create a WebSocket server (for example with the ws package) listening on its own port.

2.     Keep a map of room names to the set of sockets in each room.

3.     When a client sends a join message, store the room name on the socket and add it to that room's set.

4.     For every other message type, forward the raw message to the other sockets in the same room, and never echo it back to the sender.

5.     On disconnect, remove the socket from its room so stale peers do not receive messages.

In production you would add authentication, limit room size to two for one-to-one calls, and validate message types, but the shape stays the same.

Both sides create an RTCPeerConnection, add their local tracks and listen for the remote ones:

1.     Create a new RTCPeerConnection with an iceServers list that contains at least one STUN server.

2.     Loop over the local stream's tracks and call addTrack() for each one.

3.     In the ontrack handler, attach the first remote stream to your remote video element.

4.     In the onicecandidate handler, send every new candidate to the other peer through your signalling channel as a candidate message.

One peer creates an offer, the other answers, and both apply each other's descriptions. Candidates arrive asynchronously in the background.

1.     The caller runs createOffer(), applies it with setLocalDescription() and sends the description as an offer message.

2.     The callee applies the offer with setRemoteDescription(), runs createAnswer(), applies it locally and sends it back as an answer.

3.     The caller applies the answer with setRemoteDescription().

4.     Both sides pass every incoming candidate message to addIceCandidate().

If both peers might start a call at the same time, look up the "perfect negotiation" pattern on MDN. It assigns one side as polite and resolves offer collisions cleanly instead of leaving both peers stuck.

On a home network, STUN alone often works. On corporate Wi-Fi, mobile carriers and symmetric NATs it frequently does not, and the call silently fails to connect. A TURN server relays media when a direct path is impossible. The open-source coturn server is the usual choice.

In practice, your iceServers list should contain your own STUN URL plus a TURN URL with a username and credential, so the browser can fall back to the relay automatically when direct connectivity fails.

Generate short-lived TURN credentials on your backend rather than hard-coding them, and budget for bandwidth: relayed calls pass every frame through your server.

Users deny camera access, unplug headsets and switch from Wi-Fi to 4G mid-call. Catch NotAllowedError and show a clear explanation, listen for devicechange events, and use RTCRtpSender.replaceTrack() to swap cameras without renegotiating the whole call.

Listen to pc.onconnectionstatechange. When the state becomes disconnected, wait a few seconds before giving up, because it often recovers. On failed, call pc.restartIce() to try new network paths.

Use RTCRtpSender.setParameters() to cap the bitrate on weak connections, and read pc.getStats() to monitor packet loss and round-trip time. Showing a small "poor connection" indicator does more for perceived quality than any codec tweak.

Mesh calls, where every participant connects to every other, work for two or three people. Beyond that, upload bandwidth explodes. Group calls need a Selective Forwarding Unit (SFU) such as mediasoup, LiveKit or Janus, which receives each stream once and forwards it to the others.

Tutorials end at a working demo, but the fastest way to understand what production WebRTC looks like is to watch a real call from the inside. Chrome ships an internal diagnostics page that shows every peer connection the browser opens, and it works on any site that uses WebRTC.

1.     Open chrome://webrtc-internals in one tab and keep it open.

2.     In another tab, start a call on your own demo and find its RTCPeerConnection entry.

3.     Expand that entry and look at the ICE candidate pairs: you will see whether the call went direct (host or srflx) or through a TURN relay (relay).

4.     Open the stats graphs for the outbound video stream and watch the bitrate, frame rate and resolution adapt when you throttle the network in DevTools.

5.     Check the negotiated codec and how quickly the connection recovers when you switch Wi-Fi off and back on.

Once you can read your own call, repeat the exercise on a production service. Any browser-based product works, whether a meeting tool or a live video chat that pairs two visitors, because webrtc-internals shows the same data for every site. Expect differences from your demo: more candidate pairs, a relay candidate in the list and a bitrate that adapts faster. Seeing them makes the earlier sections concrete, from why TURN matters to how bandwidth estimation reacts to packet loss.

The one-to-one call above is the core of many products, from telehealth consultations and tutoring to customer support. The engineering challenges grow with the use case: random matching needs queueing and presence on the signalling server, public platforms need moderation and reporting, and global audiences need TURN servers in several regions.

Start with the minimal version, get it working across Chrome, Firefox and Safari on both desktop and mobile, and add TURN before real users arrive. Once the call is reliable, every other feature becomes a much easier problem.

#10508. Lawyer’s $9,450 private AI server with 12 used Nvidia V100s now runs every DeepSeek V4.1 Flash expert

He says it decodes one request faster than published DGX Spark stacks, at half the price of three.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A lawyer has upped the ante by upgrading his private AI server from eight to 12 used Nvidia V100s, which can run every DeepSeek V4.1 Flash expert at 4-bit NVFP4. Performance can reach up to 184 tokens per second for code, and long documents start answering in about half the time as before. He says it can outrun published DGX Spark stacks at decoding a single request, for half the price of three units, based on the $9,450 he paid.

Some of the best movie franchises come in threes, and Nick Mermiges seems determined to match that, taking his original setup — which he previously upgraded with more memory and other fixes — and expanding it for fuller precision, after a previous upgrade got him most of the way there. The newest upgrade, this time, involves a third board of V100s, for a total of 12 with four per board. There may be a method to his madness.

The original eight 32GB cards could manage 254 GiB, but the experts for the chosen model, with Nvidia’s NVFP4 release, need 278.9 GiB. His previous workaround was to have every expert on the cards at 2-bit precision, with the most-used, 5,949 or about 38.7%, swapped to NVFP4 until the cards were full. Now, all 15,360 experts can live on the cards in their full glory, as he intended, with “none compressed further,” according to his GitHub repo. In a longer write-up on his site, he says the expert values “equal the ones in DeepSeek’s own release,” although other parts of the model use compact forms.

A keen observer will notice a limitation of the V100s, which he spells out: the V100 has no FP4 or FP8 tensor cores. Every NVFP4 number has to be unpacked in software, first, and into half precision. The compact experts still pay off “because the cards read fewer bytes, not because they compute faster.” This helps explain why a card from 2018 can run a format from the Blackwell era.

The model’s layers are, again, divided among the boards, this time with 0–13, 14–27, and 28–39, and each board’s section is split across its own four cards. The cards on a board share Nvidia’s fast link, or NVLink, and the hand-offs between boards cross over the host’s PCIe through two separate PCIe switches. The first two boards share a PEX88096 switch, so their hand-off stays inside it, but the third board is behind a separate PLX 8749, so the second hand-off also has to cross the host’s PCIe bridges, which adds latency.

All this trouble is worth it, though, as with different settings — each card’s power cap raised from 150 W to 175 W and its clock cap lowered from 1,522 MHz to 1,452 MHz — there are noticeable improvements to performance as well as precision.

His measurements show the first code token, with 256K context, at 58 seconds, almost twice as fast as the 108.4 seconds after his previous upgrade. Prompt reading is at “about 4,500 tokens a second” past 32K, which is more than twice the roughly 2,200 tokens a second of his first build, though the higher power cap likely accounts for some of that. Code decode, meanwhile, is 23% faster at 1K, but shows no real gains at 32K and up, so the real gain is a reduced wait for the first token.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

There was some eagerness to get more out of the system by pushing each card to 200 W for a day, but “the host tripped once under four long chats at once,” implying the power draw was perhaps too much. He also worked to optimize the setup as much as possible, but four optimizations — a conversation store that saves to disk, a leaner hand-off between sections, tighter expert packing, and exact four-card-sum arithmetic — had to be turned off due to missing their targets.

Another common technique is to run multiple requests at once, usually netting a speed improvement. In this case, three requests at 128K got about 360 tokens per second in total on code and 213 on legal. That said, “this is not a batched-decode engine; more requests than sections gain little.”

However, that might be next on the docket, as he plans to study batched decoding, “the one thing the Sparks have that I want,” he wrote on Reddit. Beyond that, there are plans to turn on the conversation store so a matter’s documents can survive a restart. While he acknowledges that the numbers do not establish “answer quality in use,” the setup has been his default DeepSeek machine since Monday, and he says the model is the first one “that works like Opus 4.6” for him.

#10507. Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

I help lead Google's agent skills program, currently with 175 agent skills that supplement community collections like go-skills. To keep that many skill files consistent, I wrote skill-lint, a standalone Go CLI that checks SKILL.md files and applies LLM-generated line edits before they ship. Even with skill-lint's own unit and fuzz test suite, a small slice or line-number mistake inside its Markdown parsers or edit applier can sit dormant until an unusual file or edit range triggers a runtime panic in the linter.

In Go, every slice or index operation that can panic at runtime compiles down to an explicit bounds-check trap in machine code, after the compiler's optimizer strips out the checks it can already rule out. To hunt for crashes, I compiled key functions from my code to see which panic traps survived optimization, and Gemini 4 Argon translated the path to each trap into rules for the Z3 constraint solver. An LLM-written solver model isn't a sound proof system, but it gives the agent an independent way to reason about control flow and synthesize concrete Go reproducers, and every crash it finds is real because go test runs the offending bytes directly. That workflow caught all three bugs I planted in test parsers and turned up three latent bugs in skill-lint.

In this post, I'll walk through how the Gemini and Z3 pipeline works, why go test -fuzz missed two of those crashes (and the one-line change that fixes it), and where this approach fits in a day-to-day Go workflow.

A recent post from Anthropic showed how formal verification catches latent bugs in C and C++ by having frontier AI models write machine-checked Rocq proofs across OSS-Fuzz libraries. Instead of sampling random inputs like a fuzzer, a formal tool works backward from each failure condition to solve for the exact input that triggers it. Full theorem proving is still too heavy for most day-to-day Go projects, so I wanted to see how much of that bug-hunting power an agent can get from an LLM and a constraint solver in about a minute per function.

In C and C++, reading or writing past the end of a buffer corrupts memory. Go catches out-of-bounds access at runtime instead, compiling every slice or index operation it can't prove safe into an explicit branch to runtime.panicBounds that panics, crashing the process unless recovered.

Before generating machine code, Go's SSA prove pass already eliminates most of those checks by tracking slice lengths and loop variables. That optimization pass leaves behind only the runtime.panicBounds traps the compiler couldn't rule out on its own, giving the solver a short list of candidate crash sites to target.

I compiled each target with GOOS=linux GOARCH=riscv64 go build -gcflags="-S -d=ssa/check_bce/debug=1" (-S prints the assembly listing and ssa/check_bce/debug=1 logs every surviving bounds check) and passed both the Go source and the disassembly to the model. Looking at Go source alone, you can't see which bounds checks the compiler already removed or where a register gets truncated to 8 bits; in the disassembly, both show up. I targeted RISC-V because it's the instruction set Anthropic's post targets for its Rocq proofs, and Go cross-compiles to it out of the box.

From there, Argon read the Go source and disassembly and returned a Z3 script modeling the path to each surviving trap, along with a patched function whenever it flagged a bug. Z3 then checked whether those constraints could be satisfied, and whenever it found a crashing input, go test replayed those bytes against the real function. Using an LLM saved me from translating assembly into solver constraints by hand. Argon's translation dominated the runtime, at about a minute per function, while Z3 itself finished in milliseconds.

Anthropic's approach relies on interactive theorem proving, where experts model machine semantics and Rocq checks LLM-generated proofs against those rules. In contrast, my workflow uses a constraint solver based on satisfiability modulo theories (SMT), relying on Argon to translate assembly directly into Z3 constraints. While a constraint solver doesn't provide a complete correctness proof without a semantic model, it is highly effective for finding bugs. Z3's check-sat command returns sat when input constraints can be satisfied and unsat when they cannot.

A sat answer ("definitely buggy") comes with concrete bytes aimed at the trap, and go test runs them against the real function. Once go test panics, you have a concrete bug reproducer regardless of whether Argon's model was sound. Like a Bloom filter for bugs, an unsat answer only means "maybe correct." Trading away a correctness proof is what makes the approach so practical: you get most of the bug-finding value of formal methods for a tiny fraction of the effort.

Anthropic's setup proved no-crash properties on whole binaries under a 64 MB budget; I checked individual functions on fresh 64-byte slices where cap(buf) == len(buf), so any slice past len(buf) panics. (If you want the full-binary Rocq setup for Go, theorem-labs/oss-verify includes a Go 1.27 runtime overlay that makes the runtime single-threaded and deterministic.)

For the fuzzing baseline, I ran unseeded go test -fuzz on Go 1.27.3 against each planted bug: one 3-second run and three 60-second runs, each with a fresh fuzz cache so a short time budget wouldn't explain a miss.

I started with three safe parsers to make sure the pipeline didn't flag clean code. The compiler's prove pass removed every bounds check in a TOML key parser on its own, and Z3 returned unsat on the remaining traps in an HTTP header scanner and a varint decoder.

Then I wrote three parsers with subtle panics. Here's the first one, ParseTwoChunkFrame:

In the RISC-V disassembly, the bug is much easier to see:

total := hdrLen + bodyLen adds two uint8 values, so MOVBU wraps the sum at 256 before the length guard checks it, while endBody adds the full 64-bit ints. Argon turned those instructions into a Z3 query asking whether a fresh slice of up to 64 bytes can pass the guard and still fail the endBody check at 0x0092:

Z3 returned a 59-byte counterexample with hdrLen = 48 and bodyLen = 236: their uint8 sum wraps from 284 down to 28 (so 28 + 4 ≤ 59 passes the guard), and buf[endHdr:endBody] then slices to index 288 and panics.

At 59 bytes, 19.4% of all possible (hdrLen, bodyLen) pairs trigger this crash. Yet a 3-second fuzz run missed it, and so did three 60-second runs.

Go checks a slice expression like buf[endHdr:endBody] against the slice's capacity, not its length, and Go's fuzzer rarely passes a slice without spare capacity. In a 10-second probe of 386,741 fuzz calls, 89% of inputs were views into a shared ~100 MB buffer, and only 0.07% had cap == len. With that much spare capacity, endBody (at most 514) never exceeds cap(buf), so the buggy parser returns bytes past the end of buf instead of panicking.

The second target, ExtractQuotedToken, hit the same blind spot when a trailing backslash pushed the index one byte past len(buf). Z3 caught it immediately with the 2-byte input []byte{'"', '\\'}, while the fuzzer absorbed the overrun in spare capacity.

Clipping capacity to length with a full slice expression turns any slice past len into a panic:

After that change, the fuzzer crashed both ParseTwoChunkFrame and ExtractQuotedToken in all three runs (median 0.28 s and 0.17 s). The third target, ParseMagicPacket, was a control because its signed int8 length bug panics on a negative index regardless of capacity, and the fuzzer found it in every run either way. Fuzzing still can't give you an unsat signal across the remaining paths, which is how I checked the three safe parsers and Argon's patches.

Next, I pointed the pipeline at skill-lint itself. A whole Go CLI is too large to hand to a solver directly. Even a 10-line parser links 1,559 symbols from the Go runtime, and the 28 MB skill-lint binary has 32,321 symbols in total. Following Functional Core, Imperative Shell, I left CLI flags, network calls, and file I/O to go test -fuzz and go test -race, and ran the solver only on pure functions and slice helpers extracted from file-reading code.

Of the five functions I tested, two scanners came back unsat (including a CRLF line splitter with six surviving bounds checks), and the other three had bugs. The sharpest was in ApplyReplacementChunks, which validates LLM-generated {StartLine, EndLine} edits against the initial line count and sorts them bottom-to-top so earlier edits don't shift later line numbers:

Z3 immediately returned sat because sorting by StartLine doesn't prevent two chunks from overlapping. In the extracted helper, which checks bounds once up front, replacing lines 3 and 4 of a 4-line file with one line shrinks lines to 3 elements, so a second chunk spanning lines 2 through 4 evaluates lines[4:] and panics with slice bounds out of range [4:3]. In the shipped ApplyChunks, a per-iteration bounds check turns that case into a misleading range error, while overlapping chunks that stay within len(lines) silently overwrite the earlier edit. Rejecting overlapping chunks after the sort fixes both.

The other two bugs were similar accounting errors. In the local fixer, target line numbers were checked once per violation before the inner chunk loop applied deletions that shrank the slice (a latent panic if a fixer ever returns a second chunk after a deletion, though DeleteLineFix only returns one today). In the prompt packer, a truncation check reserved a flat 100 bytes for a ~70-byte wrapper plus file path, overshooting the 2 MB context budget in the shipped code when a path exceeds about 30 bytes and panicking in the extracted helper.

When you check a function modularly like this, the target has to include its own entry-point guards (like the bounds check and sort in ApplyReplacementChunks). If an internal helper assumes preconditions that are only enforced by its caller or stated in a doc comment, Z3 will happily invent inputs that violate them. Because each extracted helper included its entry guards, false positives weren't an issue: across all six buggy functions in my tests, the first sat assignment Z3 returned triggered a real panic in go test without needing to filter out non-crashing candidates or loop back into the solver.

Once I pulled these helpers away from file I/O, a 3-second fuzz run caught all three bugs too. To make sure Argon wasn't leaning on source comments in my test files, I also reran the skill-lint targets with all comments stripped; Argon and Z3 still caught all three bugs and returned unsat on the two scanners and all three patches.

Staying entirely inside the target language makes a whole class of bugs hard for an agent to see. When an LLM reviews Go source directly, a line like if int(total)+4 > len(buf) looks like a textbook bounds check, and when it runs go test -fuzz, target-language runtime details like spare slice capacity or multi-step precondition checks can hide the crash indefinitely. Asking the agent to translate Go and assembly into a constraint solver forces a change of representation that strips away surface syntax and makes every bit width and slice bound explicit, while go test turns any solver hit back into a concrete Go reproducer.

For most Go code, fuzzing pure helpers with slice capacity clipped to length will catch bounds bugs in seconds. A solver is worth the extra minute on the traps that survive both the compiler's range prover and a quick fuzz run, especially in parsers and editors that compute offsets from multi-field headers or line ranges.

Single-threaded solver models don't see data races or deadlocks, so concurrent code still belongs with the race detector, deterministic concurrency testing, and Go 1.27's goroutine leak profiles.

If you're experimenting with solvers or coding agents on Go, let's compare notes on X or LinkedIn.

Agent Anomaly Detection: Safeguarding agents across other clouds

Distributed LLM Training Across Metros: Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

Google introduced 3D emoji on Pixel this year, and has just detailed the minimum specifications required to get them on your Android phone.

The listed storage and RAM requirements refer to the “automatic background download via Google Play services on eligible Android 17 devices that didn’t ship with the 3D font preinstalled.” The support article did not make that distinction.

Noto 3D emoji, with its high-resolution bitmap approach, uses less than 220 MB of storage versus the previous 2D vector font that comes in at around 3 MB.

During installation, Google Play services temporarily needs a bit of extra headroom to copy the file into place before immediately clearing its download cache.

Meanwhile, Google said RAM usage is “minimal & transient” as “Android doesn’t load the 3D emoji font file into RAM.” The support page listed 6 GB as a “cutoff to ensure lower-memory devices stay on the lightweight ~3 MB 2D vector font.”

Noto 3D emoji goes from vector-based 2D emoji to a high-resolution bitmap font. This results in “high-resolution designs” for emoji “with more texture and dimension to characters and icons.” Google has an FAQ section about why the new emoji might look low resolution:

Vector vs. bitmap rendering: Older Android emoji used 2D vector graphics, which scale infinitely to any size without losing resolution. The 3D emoji are bitmap graphics, which are pre-rendered pixel-based images. When these images are scaled up by an app or zoomed in, the pixels stretch, which can cause a softer or lower-resolution appearance.

The Google support article published this week lists the minimum specs your Android device must have to get 3D emoji:

The last two bullet points are rather surprising. In fact, Google even lists instructions for checking how much memory and storage your device has:

In terms of availability, Google says a supported device will “automatically download the 3D emoji when it’s on Wi-Fi and charging.”

At this time, there isn’t a way to force download the emoji.

Google announced 3D emoji in May, with the new design shipping on the Pixel 11 in August. It has since arrived on older Pixel devices.

In terms of OEM availability, Google explicitly says “Samsung devices use a proprietary emoji font and do not receive the 3D font update.” Additionally:

This is also true for third-party messaging apps, such as WhatsApp, who use their own emoji fonts rather than relying on your device’s system font.

#10502. Building Email Integrations: APIs, Webhooks, Automation, and Deliverability

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Email remains an important communication channel for web applications. From account verification and password resets to product announcements and customer notifications, many applications depend on email to keep users informed.

For developers, however, implementing email functionality involves more than connecting a service and sending a message. A reliable email system needs to handle authentication, dynamic content, delivery failures, user preferences, event tracking, and integrations with other application components.

Using an email service provider (ESP) can simplify this process. Most modern providers offer REST APIs, SMTP relays, templates, automation features, and reporting tools that developers can integrate into existing applications.

This guide explores the technical considerations involved in building email integrations, including API-based sending, webhook handling, automation workflows, and deliverability.

There are two common ways to connect an application to an email delivery service: SMTP and REST APIs.

SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) is a standard protocol used to transfer email messages between servers. Developers can connect an application to an SMTP server using credentials and configuration settings.

REST APIs provide another approach. Instead of communicating through an email server protocol, an application sends HTTP requests to an endpoint. The provider processes the request and returns a response.

Both methods can support transactional and application-generated emails, but their implementation differs.

An application already supports SMTP configuration.

Developers want a relatively straightforward integration.

Existing frameworks or libraries provide built-in SMTP support.

An application needs programmatic control over email operations.

Developers want structured responses and error handling.

Email functionality needs to integrate with other application services.

The system requires event tracking or additional API capabilities.

The right approach depends on the application's architecture, expected email volume, and integration requirements.

An API integration allows an application to trigger emails in response to specific events.

For example, an ecommerce application might send an order confirmation after a successful payment. A SaaS application could send an account verification message after registration.

A typical integration follows this process:

The backend prepares the recipient and message data.

The backend authenticates with the email provider.

The application records the response and handles any errors.

Consider a Node.js application that needs to trigger an email campaign through an API.

Some platforms, including Mailmodo, provide REST API functionality for triggering campaigns and integrating external applications with email workflows.

The following example demonstrates the general structure of an HTTP request using a campaign-triggering endpoint:

This example uses the documented Mailmodo trigger endpoint and request structure. The campaign ID and API key must be replaced with the values from the relevant account.

Notice that the API key is loaded from an environment variable rather than written directly into the source code.

This is an important security practice. Credentials should never be exposed in frontend JavaScript, public repositories, or client-side application bundles.

Not every email provider offers the same integration capabilities. Developers should evaluate providers based on their API documentation, authentication methods, supported endpoints, event notifications, and integration flexibility.

For example, Elastic Email provides a REST API that developers can use to send emails and manage different email operations from an application.

Its API uses HTTPS requests and API-key authentication. Developers can use the available endpoints to send messages, manage campaigns, retrieve information, and configure webhook notifications.

When evaluating an email API, consider these technical requirements:

Authentication: Check how API keys are generated, stored, restricted, and rotated.

Documentation: Look for clear endpoint descriptions, request examples, response formats, and error codes.

SDK availability: Official libraries can reduce the amount of integration code required.

Permission management: Separate credentials for sending emails and accessing account information can reduce unnecessary exposure.

API limitations: Review connection limits, request timeouts, rate limits, and account-level restrictions before deploying an integration.

A provider with extensive features is not necessarily the best option for every project. The important consideration is whether its API supports the application's actual requirements.

Sending an email is only one part of the communication process. Applications may also need to know what happens after a message is submitted.

A webhook allows an external service to send an HTTP notification to an application when a particular event occurs.

Depending on the provider, events may include:

For example, an application could receive a delivery-failure notification and update the corresponding email record in its database.

A basic Express.js webhook endpoint might look like this:

This is a simplified example rather than a complete provider-specific webhook implementation. A production endpoint should validate the provider's signature or other authentication mechanism and follow its documented payload structure.

It should also handle duplicate events safely. Webhook providers may retry notifications when an application does not respond successfully.

An idempotent event handler prevents the same notification from creating duplicate database updates or triggering repeated actions.

Email automation connects application events with predefined communication sequences.

Instead of manually sending every message, developers can create workflows that respond to user activity.

Consider a SaaS application with the following registration process:

If the user does not complete onboarding, a follow-up message may be triggered.

A simplified workflow can be represented as:

In a production application, email delivery should generally be handled through a background job queue rather than blocking the registration request.

A queue also makes it easier to retry temporary failures, manage sending volume, and prevent email delays from affecting the main application.

Developers should consider tools such as Redis-backed queues or framework-specific job processors when building larger systems.

Automation platforms can also help manage more complex journeys. For instance, a developer can connect application events to an external email workflow instead of implementing every communication sequence independently.

The key is to keep business logic in the application while using the email service to manage message delivery and relevant campaign operations.

A successful API response does not necessarily mean that an email has reached the recipient's inbox.

Email deliverability depends on several technical factors, including domain authentication, sender reputation, message quality, and recipient engagement.

Developers should pay attention to the following configurations.

Sender Policy Framework identifies the mail servers authorized to send messages on behalf of a domain.

A correctly configured SPF record helps receiving servers evaluate whether an email originates from an authorized source.

DomainKeys Identified Mail adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages. Receiving servers can use the corresponding public key in DNS to verify the signature.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance builds on SPF and DKIM. It allows domain owners to publish policies for handling messages that fail authentication checks and receive reports about authentication activity.

These three mechanisms should be configured carefully, especially when an application sends emails through multiple providers.

Email systems generate useful operational data. However, developers should avoid treating every available metric as an accurate measure of user engagement.

More reliable application-level signals can include:

These events can be connected with email campaign data to understand how communication affects application activity.

Developers should also maintain a clear separation between email delivery records and user account records. This makes it easier to investigate delivery issues without unnecessarily modifying customer data.

Before deploying an email integration, developers should review the following areas:

API security: Store credentials in environment variables or a dedicated secrets manager.

Domain authentication: Configure SPF, DKIM, and DMARC.

Error handling: Account for failed requests, invalid recipients, and temporary provider errors.

Background processing: Use queues for asynchronous email delivery.

Webhook validation: Verify incoming notifications and handle duplicate events.

Monitoring: Track delivery failures, bounce rates, and application-level outcomes.

Testing: Test email templates across different clients and devices before production deployment.

These practices help create an email system that is easier to maintain, troubleshoot, and scale.

Email integration is an important part of modern application development, but a reliable implementation requires more than connecting an SMTP server or calling an API endpoint.

Developers need to consider authentication, asynchronous processing, webhook security, automation logic, domain configuration, and event monitoring.

REST APIs are useful for applications that need programmatic control over email operations, while SMTP remains a practical option for systems that already support standard mail-server connections. Webhooks add another layer by allowing applications to respond to delivery events and update their internal records.

By choosing an integration method that fits the application architecture and following sound security and deliverability practices, developers can build email functionality that supports both immediate communication needs and long-term application growth.

It seems that controversial questions are frowned upon.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A Bloomberg reporter was ejected from a historic trilateral meeting between the leaders of the United States, China, and Russia after reportedly asking a sensitive question to the U.S. President while in the presence of the leader of the Chinese Communist Party. According to The Washington Post, Bloomberg News journalist Annmarie Hordern, the chief political correspondent and co-host of the media outlet’s leading program, Bloomberg Surveillance, asked, “President Trump, has Jensen Huang been disloyal to the United States?”

A second reporter had then started asking a second question before Chinese officials shushed the group. It’s unclear if Trump was able to answer both these questions. Senior White House Correspondent for Fox News Jacqui Heinrich said on X that China, the host country of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit where the trilateral meeting was happening along the side, told journalists that “yelling questions is considered extremely rude” and that anyone doing so “will be pulled from the room,” possibly implying the nature of the questions' delivery, rather than its content, was behind the decision.

Jensen Huang’s sudden resignation as the CEO of Nvidia has been a major scandal in the past few weeks. Even though he admitted to having an affair, he did not name the third party, reported to be Alice Huang, a Chinese executive for Megaspeed who’s also accused of diverting $7.5 billion worth of Nvidia chips into China and passing along information to Chinese intelligence.

The U.S. and China have been locked in a rivalry in recent years, with artificial intelligence being one of the industries that the Chinese Communist Party is focusing on. This race to achieve “supremacy” in AI has led the White House to impose export controls on its most powerful chips and to bar hardware containing U.S. tech from making its way into China. But even though Trump made a 180-degree turn and finally allowed Nvidia H100 chips into China in December 2025, provided the company can secure export licenses, the most advanced Blackwell and upcoming Vera Rubin chips from Nvidia are still out of reach for Chinese companies. Aside from that, Washington is also closing a loophole that allowed Chinese AI firms to take advantage of American compute by exporting their data to data centers located in other countries. Despite all those controls, rumors of AI chip smuggling still persist, with several individuals, including Supermicro co-founder Yih-Shyan “Wally” Liaw, getting arrested for chip diversion.

China also puts tighter control on its journalists and censors sensitive topics. As the host of the APEC summit and the trilateral meeting, it could exclude reporters that it thinks violate its standards. Heinrich said that the White House advocated for Hordern’s inclusion in the trilateral meeting, but it seems that the Chinese refused to budge on their decision.

Hacks across Europe caused supermarket fridges to reach temperatures of 50 degrees Celsius, but a repeat of the issue isn't expected in the U.K.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Following devastating hacks that caused European fridges to reach 50 degrees Celsius, destroying food stocks, the UK has implemented nationwide patching to ensure similar attacks won't affect its fridges. While it was thought that the UK had avoided similar hacks because it used different devices from those used in mainland Europe, an even worse issue was just waiting to be discovered. The BBC reports that a different flaw was discovered by security researchers in late October, one that could have caused even worse damage if it had been identified by hackers. Since then, work has been carried out to harden security across the U.K., with fridges in supermarkets, warehouses, and elsewhere now protected.

Refrigeration consultants Omega Solutions were the first to identify the issue before the National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), the Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs (Defra) and Food Standards Agency (FSA) all became involved in working on the problem. As a precaution, thousands of devices that control tens of thousands of fridges were blocked from accessing the internet, with supermarket IT workers scrambling to sometimes physically isolate devices by removing network cables.

"From a business perspective, it could have been potentially devastating with business interruption and supply chain problems. Our warehouses have millions of pounds worth of food that could have been spoiled if any hackers found out about this method of attack," one supermarket worker warned.

The issue that they were trying to address rested within the most popular refrigeration controllers used across the U.K., made by British firm Resource Data Management (RDM). It was found that these controllers had a similar authentication security flaw to those hacked across Europe, although RDM points out that hacking its devices would have required a specific piece of hardware. However, that hardware is freely available.

RDM has since confirmed that there was no sign of any malicious activity on any of its controllers throughout the patching process.

The situation in France, Belgium, and other European countries was much more dire. After initially hitting France in October, a further wave of attacks swept through supermarkets and even blood banks. Even the fridges at a U.S. military base were not safe. While no responsibility for the attacks has been taken, France has so far laid the blame at the door of Iran — something the country's government denies.
