#10492. How to Test WCAG 2.2 in CI: What axe-core Misses

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Your accessibility check in CI is green. axe-core ran, zero violations, and the badge in the README looks great. Your site can still fail WCAG 2.2.

That isn't a bug in axe. WCAG 2.2 became a W3C Recommendation in October 2023. It added nine success criteria and dropped one (4.1.1 Parsing), and most of the new ones can't be judged by reading the DOM. Deque said as much when it shipped the first WCAG 2.2 support in axe-core 4.5: the target-size rule would probably be the only new-criterion rule they added, because the others can't be automated without a lot of false positives.

So the rest is on you. This article covers the one criterion axe can check and three it can't (focus not obscured, dragging movements, accessible authentication), with Playwright tests you can drop into a project. I ran all of them against a small test page that fails every criterion on purpose, and against a fixed version of the same page. If you want a plain-language rundown of every change first, WCAG 2.2 vs 2.1 goes through them. Here I only care about what you can put in a pipeline.

Level A and AA are what most contracts and laws point at, so those are the ones that matter first. The AAA criteria are still worth knowing about.

That's four AA criteria with something you can script, one of which axe already handles. The rest of this article goes through those four.

The AA rule is smaller than most people expect: a pointer target should be at least 24 by 24 CSS pixels, or sit far enough from its neighbours that a 24px circle centred on it wouldn't overlap another target or that target's own circle. There are exceptions, including inline links in a sentence, and targets whose size the browser controls.

axe-core has a target-size rule for this. One catch: it isn't part of a default run. In my setup (axe-core 4.14.1 through @axe-core/playwright 4.14.0), a plain analyze() on the broken page reported nothing at all. The rule only showed up once I asked for it:

On the broken page this lists the three 16px toolbar buttons by selector. On the fixed page, with 32px buttons, it passes. If you've been running axe for years and never seen a target-size result, this is why.

Some context on how common the problem is. In the scan data behind my last SitePoint article, 34.5% of the 1,025 sites had controls below a stricter 44px threshold. That is a tougher standard than the 24px that AA asks for, so don't read it as "a third of sites fail 2.5.8". Plenty of those would pass. But it does show how many production sites ship tiny controls.

If you'd like a quick look at one page before wiring anything up, browser tools such as axe DevTools, WAVE, or a WCAG checker will show the same thing.

axe can't test it, because you have to press Tab and look at where the browser scrolled. Two details caught me out building this:

The check below samples a grid of points inside the visible part of the focused element and asks the browser what's on top at each one. If none of the points land on the element or its children, it's covered.

On my broken page it reported nine hits: links hidden behind the footer going forward ("Section 4 link", "Section 10 link", "Section 16 link") and the toolbar buttons and a few links hidden behind the header coming back. The output names the element and the key, which is what you want when you're staring at a failed build.

The fix is one CSS rule, sized to your sticky elements:

That tells the browser to leave room when it scrolls a focused element into view. With it in place the same test passes.

Be aware of what this can't see: a modal that opens over the page, or a chat widget that only appears after a delay, will not show up unless your test opens or waits for them. Adrian Roselli's piece on 2.4.11 and adversarial conformance is worth reading for the awkward cases.

Anything you can do by dragging needs a way to do it with a single click or tap, unless dragging is essential. Sortable lists, sliders, kanban cards, file reorderers: all of them.

You can't prove a drag alternative works from a script. But you can catch the obvious mistake, which is a draggable item with no button anywhere near it:

The limit is real. It only recognises the native draggable attribute. Libraries built on pointer events, such as dnd-kit, don't set it, so this test will walk right past them. If you use one of those, replace the selector with whatever your component renders, or add a test per drag component. Treat it as a tripwire for the common case, not a verdict.

The fix, in the fixed page, is a pair of "Move up" and "Move down" buttons on each item, each with an accessible name that says which item it moves.

You can't make someone solve a cognitive test to log in (remember a password, transcribe a code) unless there's an alternative or a mechanism that helps. Password managers count as that mechanism, which means the two ways sites break it are blocking paste and omitting the right autocomplete values.

This dispatches a synthetic paste event and checks whether anything called preventDefault() on it. It catches the old onpaste="return false" trick, and it catches script-based blockers too. It does not tell you whether your login has a CAPTCHA that needs an alternative, or an emailed one-time code you must retype. Those need a person.

On a pull request, a plain GitHub Actions job is enough:

Point PAGE_URL at a running preview, and run the tests against the pages that matter most: sign-in, checkout, anything with a sticky element. Don't try to crawl everything on the first pass. A test that fails for reasons nobody owns gets deleted within a month.

Four things I haven't tried to automate, and wouldn't:

Automation will keep the regressions out. It won't tell you the work is finished. A green build and a conformant site are different things, and WCAG 2.2 widened the gap between them.

The tests and both test pages are small enough to read in one sitting. Copy what's useful, change the selectors, and keep the broken page around: it's a handy way to check that a test fails when it should.

About the author: [name] builds WCAGsafe, an accessibility scanner. The study data above comes from its scans; the code in this article is independent of it and runs against any page.

#10489. Phison details SSD with a built-in NPU and 64GB of memory that supports AI frameworks on the drive

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Neither design has a product name, a named controller or a sample date yet.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Phison detailed two new enterprise SSD designs at Supercomputer 2026 (SC26) that aim to answer two separate problems: one with a built-in NPU and 64GB of 130 GB/s memory that supports running PyTorch, vLLM, OpenVINO, and llama.cpp directly on the drive, and another that merges fast pSLC cache with large QLC capacity behind just one controller. The company notes that “many of the products and features mentioned are still in development” and will arrive once finalized.

The computational-storage design’s premise is that a server can offload more to the SSD, which runs AI code on the data where it already resides and sends back results, instead of whole datasets. The pSLC-QLC design, meanwhile, puts the cache inside each drive, which may reduce system complexity, as every drive bay contributes usable capacity and none goes to dedicated cache drives.

Phison says that the integrated NPU is expected to deliver “36,800 DMIPS of compute performance,” or Dhrystone Million Instructions Per Second, a typical synthetic benchmark. This is admittedly an old CPU integer benchmark from the 1980s, while NPUs tend to be rated in TOPS, but that figure was not given. For reference, the Topaz NPU add-in card Phison showed at Computex carried eight NPUs rated at up to 40 TOPS each. The DMIPS figure therefore likely refers to, instead, the CPU cores that work alongside it, which we would expect to be based on an Arm Cortex design, adequate for running an SSD of this class but no measure of its AI capability.

The memory and its bandwidth are more important for the expected workload, anyway, and while the company doesn’t state the type, LPDDR5X is a popular option and may be used with the Topaz NPU, according to one secondary report. If the drive is a PCIe 6.0 part comparable to Phison’s X3 controller, it could hit up to 28 GB/s to the host, which would put the memory above 4x that speed. For reference, 130 GB/s is a bit less than half of that of the DGX Spark’s hardware, designed for AI development.

Phison says developers can build on the drive “without specialized programming tools,” which, in this case, is a good thing. The drive does, of course, support multiple general-purpose inference servers and runtimes, but nothing was disclosed about how a host reaches the drive’s compute. The drives “scan and process data in parallel,” which indicates scalability, and return “relevant results” which Phison claims could mean 10 to 100 times less data transferred for applicable workloads. This may free up the host CPU for other tasks.

Phison’s idea to put compute on storage isn’t new. Samsung tried this with its SmartSSD — where an FPGA sat on the drive to process data without moving it — which was shown in 2018. Later, in 2022, the design was refined with AMD’s Xilinx Versal FPGA in its second generation, with Samsung claiming more than 50% lower processing time, up to 70% lower energy consumption, and up to 97% lower server CPU usage for scan-heavy database queries, compared to conventional SSDs. The drive has reportedly since disappeared from Samsung’s lineup, though the first-generation model was still sold under AMD’s Xilinx name as of last year.

ScaleFlux took a narrower route, with compression handled inside the SSD controller. The server doesn’t see this, so the drive looks like any other NVMe SSD, and no application has to change its behavior. The CEO claims it is the only company with computational storage in production at hyperscalers. This suggests that not making customers write new code for the drive is the secret to success, as SmartSSD needed that for its FPGA and for each workload. Phison’s selling point here is flexibility for the client to use code written for common AI frameworks.

Company CTO Sebastien Jean, writing in All About Circuits in 2025, argued that generic computational storage had stalled because “each use case is highly unique and it’s simply not scalable.” He called the idea of running the Linux operating system on a drive with bigger processors a “pie-in-the-sky approach.”

Accelerators have long been widely used in data centers, and Jean’s proposal was to put targeted ones inside the SSD, each running a set operation over a range of logical block addresses (LBAs). By his reckoning, a chassis with 90 PCIe 6.0 SSDs could process data at 2.5 TB/s without affecting the CPU. The design revealed at SC26, though, appears closer to the general-purpose model, possibly due to the increasing relevance of AI and the demand for both memory and compute.

Jean also spoke at the Emerging Storage Technology panel at the conference to discuss future developments, including PCIe 7.0 drives that would operate at “50, 56 gigabytes per second,” which he called “faster than a DDR channel.” With 32 drives at this speed, a single chassis could reach “1.8 terabytes per second.” He described this specifically as computational storage for HPC.

Turning to the Mixed Media solution, that’s the “high-endurance pSLC and high-capacity QLC flash” SSD; it’s a 16-channel part, so not comparable to anything you see in retail. The pSLC portion of the drive has the 4-bit QLC flash act in a 1-bit SLC mode, which trades space for performance and endurance. Phison says the design builds on standard NVMe interfaces and fits into existing infrastructure “without requiring specialized host software.”

Jean elaborated at the same panel that the “endurance group on the drive, which is SLC,” is managed separately from the XLC — 3-bit TLC or 4-bit QLC — and each region gets a namespace, so it appears as multiple drives to the OS. Then, high-write data can be sent specifically to the SLC group, while data in the QLC is likely to be more read-heavy. This follows a general trend of enterprise shifting toward QLC for capacity, but having some of it in SLC mode can help reduce the need for specialized caching.

Right now, high-capacity SSDs usually have separate SSDs to fulfill this role, but that takes up more drive bays. The tradeoff with Phison’s approach is that a single drive failure takes out both capacity and a share of the cache. That said, Phison’s approach is more robust from a cache perspective, as “losing one drive out of 24 would result in a 4% cache loss instead of a 25% loss,” the company says, with the 25% presumably assuming four dedicated cache drives.

Dedicated cache drives need tiering handled by the host or array software, while with Mixed Media each drive holds both tiers and the host simply picks the namespace it wants to write to.

The company is not the first to have this idea, either, as Kioxia presented its Mixed Mode idea during FMS 2025, where each drive has a ratio of pSLC to QLC, set once per drive when it’s first used. Kioxia called 1:4 a “good beginning ratio,” though it said customers typically want pSLC at just 0.5% to 2% of maximum QLC capacity. That implementation also had parity protection on the pSLC space across multiple drives. Kioxia similarly marketed the benefit of needing fewer slots and field-replaceable units (FRUs), and its slides even used the term “Mixed Media” in two chart headings.

DapuStor went further with its dual-mode J5060, a QLC SSD with a pSLC region that was in customer validation as of August; it compared the design against “four dedicated SLC drives” in a 24-drive system, the kind of setup that would produce Phison’s 25% figure. Solidigm’s Cloud Storage Acceleration Layer works differently, with a host-side flash translation layer, where it can manage small writes on SLC or TLC drives before writing to QLC in favorable large sequential chunks.

There’s also the Enmotus FuzeDrive P200, previewed at CES 2020 as the MiDrive, which used a Phison controller and seems to be the closest to an ancestor of the current Mixed Media. That consumer drive, which we reviewed in 2021, had SLC and QLC portions that acted distinctly, but they were managed by Enmotus software.

In every drive, behind one 16-channel controller

Separate NVMe endurance groups assigned to namespaces; no specialized host software, Phison says

In every drive, with parity protection on the pSLC space across drives

1:4 as a “good beginning ratio”; customers typically want pSLC at 0.5% to 2% of maximum QLC capacity

Ratio set once, creating a pSLC namespace and endurance group

In every drive, as a pSLC region beside the QLC

400GB, 800GB or 1.2TB of pSLC on a 30.72TB drive

Separate SLC and QLC block devices on one drive

In the drive, as a fixed SLC portion of a consumer QLC SSD

Reference system: three 800GB SLC drives and seven 61.44TB QLC drives

Flash translation layer running on the host

It’s also inevitable that these two approaches will be compared to Phison’s other upcoming part, the X3 enterprise SSD controller, a PCIe 6.0 design promising up to 28 GB/s of sequential throughput and 6.8 million IOPS. Those figures are unchanged since Computex in June.

The X3’s IOPS sounds like a lot, but we’ve heard considerable banter about AI needing way more than that, as Nvidia’s roadmap target for PCIe 7.0 is at 100 million IOPS, though that target counts 512-byte reads, a smaller unit than the 4KB that SSD ratings usually use, so the two figures aren’t directly comparable. Jean said at the panel that Phison is “very optimistic” it can achieve that. He added that the eightfold jump in IOPS that comes with moving from 4KB to 512-byte accesses would, on existing NAND, mean “eight times as much power,” or “roughly in the 140 to 180 watt range,” which is beyond reasonable, so perhaps a different solution will be required.

For the X3, Phison told us in June to expect customer samples by the end of the year, with volume coming in the middle of next year, though the SC26 release gives no dates. Notably, Micron and Samsung are already offering PCIe 6.0 SSDs, and the Bravera SC6 controller is expected from Marvell. Phison also says its PS7161, which its site lists as a customer sample, is “the first PCIe 6.0 redriver on the PCI-SIG Integrators List.”

Of the two designs, Mixed Media looks like the nearer product. It builds on standard NVMe features, and DapuStor’s similar dual-mode drive reached customer testing this summer, while the one processor figure given for the computational-storage drive says little about what it can do for AI. What remains to be seen for Mixed Media is its pSLC-to-QLC ratio and how configurable it is. CES and Computex are the notable dates on the 2027 calendar to find out more about both, as well as the X3.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

#10484. Unlocking Stable On-Policy RL on TPU with Bitwise-Zero Train–Inference Mismatch

Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is notoriously sensitive to numerical instability: even tiny discrepancies between rollout generation and gradient updates can destabilize training or stall convergence. In LLM RL, the model serves as the policy, π(action | previous tokens), and true on-policy learning requires every action in an update to be generated and evaluated by that exact same policy. While a synchronous loop ensures each rollout batch is scored using the same weights that generated it, a critical numerical requirement remains: the sampler and trainer must evaluate that policy identically.

In practice, production RL stacks run sampling and training through separate, specialized execution paths. The sampler chooses each action token during incremental decoding and records its behavior logprob. The trainer evaluates the same token in batched forward-backward passes to compute its current logprob and gradients. Under the same weights, these differing execution paths can still assign different logprobs to the same token and context. The training schedule is on-policy, but the numerical execution has introduced a hidden off-policy gap that compounds over multi-turn trajectories.

Our zero train–inference mismatch (Zero-TIM) implementation, developed in Tunix and verified at multi-host scale across 64 TPU v5p chips (DP8×TP8), closes this numerical gap. At the same weights, the sampler and trainer assign bitwise-identical logprobs to the same action and token history—bringing state-of-the-art, true on-policy stability to TPU RL training.

A reduction is a tree of floating-point additions. Floating-point addition is not associative, so changing the tree changes where rounding occurs. The effect is easier to expose in bfloat16, which retains less precision than float32.

The following bfloat16 example isolates the effect of reduction order:

The same issue appears in model reductions: matrix products accumulate partial sums, normalization computes statistics, and tensor-parallel shards combine partial outputs. In our original stack, sampler decode and trainer forward used different token-array shapes and forward implementations. JAX specializes computations to their input shapes, and the training forward also runs inside a differentiated program.

Across these paths, reduction order and intermediate precision were not consistently aligned. Floating-point rounding turned those arithmetic differences into different logprob bits. The weights and model equations were identical; the arithmetic was not.

The contract is simple: across sampler decode, serving prefill, and trainer forward, all three paths must produce bitwise-identical logprobs for every sampled action—comparing raw floating-point bytes under the same rollout weights, token history, and sampling settings, with zero rounding or tolerance.

Why three paths instead of two? In a decoupled RL stack, a direct sampler–trainer disagreement shows that a numerical gap exists, but not where it enters. Introducing serving prefill as a middle checkpoint turns alignment into a systematic two-step diagnostic that any RL stack can adopt:

Sampler decode vs. prefill verifies that the serving engine agrees with itself between incremental token generation and full-sequence re-scoring, while prefill vs. trainer verifies that the serving and training engines agree across compilation and differentiation boundaries.

Achieving Zero-TIM does not require merging the sampler and trainer into a single monolithic engine. Instead, we use a generalizable four-part recipe that bridges separate serving and training pipelines across multi-host TPU meshes: locking within-shard kernel accumulation, ordering cross-shard reductions, standardizing the logprob scoring tail, and wrapping serving kernels with custom differentiation rules.

Inside each tensor-parallel rank, batch-invariant Pallas kernels control accumulation order and intermediate precision. Projection kernels traverse fixed contraction blocks in order, keeping the reduction blocking independent of token-row count. Partial products accumulate in float32, with explicit output casts.

The production kernel initializes its scratch accumulator before the tile traversal, then applies this core to each tile:

The next tile continues from the float32 accumulator, not the cast output. This construction covers the attention and MLP projections and the LM head. Dedicated kernels fix RMSNorm reductions and SwiGLU’s intermediate casts. The existing Pallas paged-attention forward is retained.

For row-parallel projections, each tensor-parallel rank produces a partial output. We separate communication from addition: ppermute transports the partials, then every rank adds them in the same source-rank order.

The reduction order is explicit in the program rather than selected by a collective implementation.

Alignment must extend beyond model logits to the probability of the sampled action. Decode, prefill, and trainer apply matching sampling transforms, use the same fixed Pallas log-softmax construction, and select the recorded action token.

Prefill and trainer score the action generated during rollout rather than choosing another action. We also preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns so that every path evaluates the same token history.

The trainer reuses the serving prefill computation. Custom vector–Jacobian products retain the serving kernels for forward evaluation and provide their backward rules. The matmul example shows this separation:

The differentiable replica mirrors the kernel’s accumulation and casts, but is used only to construct the pullback. Forward values still come from the serving Pallas kernel.

Training places that forward inside a differentiated JAX program. We pass --xla_allow_excess_precision=false through XLA_FLAGS to the compiler process, constraining excess intermediate precision in the operations surrounding the Pallas kernels.

Long trajectories are evaluated in fixed-size chunks, carrying the paged KV cache forward. All action logprobs feed the complete train-batch RL loss. The reverse pass propagates gradients through those cache connections and combines parameter-gradient contributions before the optimizer update.

Across the AI systems community, eliminating train–inference mismatch has rapidly become a state-of-the-art requirement for stabilizing large-scale RL convergence. Recent open-source efforts—including vLLM with TorchTitan and RadixArk/LMSys Miles—have demonstrated how batch-invariant kernels and bitwise numerical alignment prevent off-policy drift during policy optimization.

Our work delivers an open-source, modular JAX/XLA and Pallas implementation of this contract for TPU in Tunix. Verified across a multi-host 64 TPU v5p (DP8×TP8) mesh, these kernel and collective patterns provide a reusable blueprint that teams can adopt to achieve bit-exact on-policy RL across distributed JAX/TPU stacks.

To benchmark our TPU kernels, we run a 200-step numerical verification test on the synthetic FrozenLake environment using a public Qwen3-8B checkpoint as a baseline test case using 64 TPU v5p chips with a DP8×TP8 mesh. The recipe uses a GSPO-token loss with leave-one-out advantages. We compare Standard training with trainer-old logprobs and no additional sampler correction, token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS), and Zero-TIM.

The Zero-TIM configuration also uses token-in/token-out (TiTO) handling to preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns. The comparison runs retain their existing tokenization paths.

Zero-TIM records zero mean absolute sampler–trainer logprob difference at every logged step in this window, while its training solve rate increases. Standard and TIS retain nonzero differences: TIS adjusts their contribution to the update without removing the underlying mismatch. Zero-TIM reaches higher observed solve rates in these runs.

We developed Zero-TIM prototype as part of Tunix, Google's open-source, JAX-native library for LLM RL and post-training on Cloud TPU. The kernel patterns and three-way verification contract are modular by design—making them straightforward to adopt across existing multi-host JAX/TPU serving and training pipelines—and we are actively extending support to linear-attention and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Agent Anomaly Detection: Safeguarding agents across other clouds

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

Newegg even sweetens the pot with a free 240mm MSI AIO ($85 value). You’re only a couple of parts away from a complete AM5 system with this awesome Newegg deal.

The RAM included in the combo is V-Color Manta XSky. This memory kit has been a commonplace in Newegg bundles for a while now. And though some may not recognize the name, they are quality kits. The sticks sport black heat spreaders and a frosted RGB element across the top and match well with the Asus board. Specs-wise, it’s a 32GB (2x16GB) DDR5-6000 kit with tight timings (CL30-38-38-96 @ 1.35V). Under the hood are SK Hynix ICs, so you do have some headroom if you want to overclock past their rated speed or to tighten them down. AMD’s sweet spot (Price/performance) for AM5 processors is around 6000-6400 MT/s, so these fall into that bucket and pair perfectly with the 9950X3D.

Save big with this 4-item Newegg combo. You get one of the most powerful X3D chips in the Ryzen 9 9950X3D processor (great for gaming and productivity), 32GB of V-Color Manta XSky DDR5-6000 with a low CL30 rating, a solid Asus ROG Strix X870E-E Gaming Wifi motherboard, and a fast 2TB Samsung 990 Pro PCIe 4 x4 drive with speeds to 7,450/6,900 MB/s (R/W). With this combo, you're either getting the RAM for only $110 or the Samsung storage for only $30. Either way, it's a beefy discount for quality components.

Next is the Ryzen 9 9950X3D processor. Before the X3D2 was released in April of this year, this was the flagship X3D SKU and offered not only a lot of cores and threads (16c/32t) but also X3D cache (128MB) on one of the CCDs, which is great for gaming. Clock speeds start at 4.3 GHz, with turbo boosting a couple of cores up to 5.7 GHz. With its clock speeds, core count, and extra cache, this processor is not only great for gaming but also awesome for any PC work that needs more cores and threads, like video editing, 3D rendering, and even running VMs.

The third part of the combo is the Samsung 990 Pro M.2. This PCIe 4.0 x4 SSD delivers up to 7,250/6,450 MB/s sequential read/write speeds and up to 850K/1,200K IOPS for random reads/writes. This is a very speedy PCIe 4 drive, only eclipsed by more costly PCIe 5-based drives. We reviewed this drive when it was released and loved its strong all-around performance, excellent software (Samsung Magician), and consistent, efficient, cool-running performance.

Last but certainly not least, we reviewed the Asus ROG Strix X870E-E Gaming Wifi motherboard and gave it four stars as one of our best options around the ~$500 price point. This premium offering gives users everything they want out of a motherboard. It has strong power delivery to support the 9950X3D processor, five M.2 sockets (three PCIe 5.0) and four SATA ports for storage, fast networking with 5GbE and 320MHz Wi-Fi, ample USB ports on the rear I/O including dual USB4 (40 Gbps) Type-C ports, and a premium audio solution using the Real ALC4080 codec along with an integrated amplifier. On top of that, it’s packed with “AI” features (Overclocking, Cooling, Cache Boost) and sports a DIY-friendly design.

If you were waiting for Black Friday week to take the pain out of buying PC components, that time is now. Even if you weren't, that time is still probably now. This 4-item combo with its huge $369 discount is only $1549 and one of the best bundles Newegg has going for those looking for a high-end entry into the AM5 platform and who need good storage too. The combo only lasts until supplies run out, so jump on this stellar deal while you can.

#10481. Microsoft is shutting down the ‘To Do’ app that replaced Wunderlist, with no real replacement

Microsoft announced this week that its To Do app is shutting down soon, taking the legacy of Wunderlist with it.

Microsoft is shutting down its To Do app at the end of June 2027, with focus moving to the newer Planner app. The company explains:

As we bring personal task management into Planner, we will phase out To Do. The To Do apps will retire on June 30, 2027. Connections that other apps use to access To Do tasks will continue beyond that date, and we’ll provide separate notice before those connections are retired. We’re also updating existing task integrations across Microsoft 365 to connect with Planner, so you’ll be able to create or manage tasks in the Microsoft experiences you already use. Although To Do tasks are already visible in Planner, they will not be migrated to the new private plan. Customers using personal Microsoft accounts will need to choose another app. Our To Do transition guidance  will help you understand what’s changing and plan your next steps.

To Do was built on the foundation of Wunderlist, a wildly popular to do list app that Microsoft purchased in 2015. Wunderlist later shut down in 2020.

Microsoft’s shuffle, notably, leaves general consumers without a replacement app. To Do was available to anyone with a Microsoft account, where Planner is available only to enterprise/business users. So, for long-time Wunderlist users who followed Microsoft migration, this is the end of the road and it’s officially time to move to another app.

#10479. Hostinger VPS in 2026: Hands-On With the AI-Managed VPS

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, renting a VPS meant signing up to be a part-time sysadmin. You’d SSH into a blank Ubuntu prompt and lose a Saturday to Nginx configs and firewall rules before writing a single line of your actual project.

In 2026, three things have changed. What we build has changed: AI coding tools turn weekend ideas into working apps, and AI agents need a machine that stays on after your laptop closes. Who runs servers has changed: you can now ask an AI agent in your terminal to set up a reverse proxy, close a port or work out why something broke. And what servers cost has changed: the AI boom is soaking up the world’s memory supply, and hosting prices are rising with it.

So I bought the smallest Hostinger VPS plan, deployed a Node.js app in Docker, and handed the DevOps work to Hostinger Agent, the AI assistant built into the dashboard.

Below is what that felt like step by step and whether it’s worth locking in a price before they climb further.

Most VPS hosting for developers now falls into four buckets. They all run on the same kind of machine; what changes is how much RAM you give them.

Apps built with AI coding tools. Claude Code and Cursor get you to a working Node.js or Python app fast, then leave you wondering where to put it. A VPS gives you one fixed monthly bill instead of usage-based pricing. If you want push-to-deploy, Hostinger offers Ubuntu templates with Coolify or Dokploy preinstalled.

AI agents and automations that run 24/7. For most people, a “VPS for AI” means a CPU box that never sleeps, not a GPU. Think n8n workflows, a scheduled scraper, or your own agent in a Docker container. Hostinger even has an “Ubuntu 24.04 with Claude Code” template: SSH in, run claude /login, and the agent keeps working after you close your laptop.

APIs, backends and databases for side projects. A Postgres instance, a Redis cache and a small API behind a reverse proxy fit comfortably on a mid-sized plan. You pick the versions, the extensions and the backup schedule.

Client sites and stores. Freelancers outgrow shared hosting when one client needs a custom PHP extension or a WooCommerce store starts to crawl. A VPS with cPanel or Plesk, or a WordPress or WooCommerce template, keeps client work isolated and under your control.

If you’d rather not build anything from scratch, the Docker Manager catalog listed 1,017 one-click templates when I checked, including 93 under AI/ML and 162 under Developer tools.

For this Hostinger VPS review, the question I cared about was simple: how long from “Choose plan” to an app on the internet? On KVM 1, the entry plan, it was about 12 minutes. Here’s each step with rough timings.

I went with KVM 1: 1 vCPU, 4 GB RAM, 50 GB NVMe storage and 4 TB of bandwidth. On the Black Friday deal it costs $6.49/mo, paid upfront as $155.76 for 24 months, and renews at $11.99/mo.

You choose the server’s location and what to install during checkout, alongside your billing details. The location picker shows estimated latency for each data centre; mine showed about 80 ms.

Install options come in three tabs: Plain OS, Control panel and Applications. I picked plain Ubuntu 26.04 LTS to see how much work the AI would take off my hands. A daily backup add-on is offered, but weekly backups are already included.

The five-minute estimate was about right. The “you’re ready” screen then gives you two ways in: the VPS dashboard, or a pre-filled ssh root@ command. It also offers a free domain for a year to use as the server’s hostname.

The overview shows the OS, plan, CPU, RAM, disk and bandwidth at a glance, with Docker Manager, Backups & Monitoring, Security and DNS Manager in the side menu.

Every server also gets a hostname under hstgr.cloud (mine was srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud), which came in handy later for SSL. I’ve masked my hostname as srvXXXXXXX throughout and blurred IP addresses in the screenshots.

Plain Ubuntu doesn’t ship with Docker. Open Docker Manager in hPanel and it installs Docker for you with one click. From there you can deploy apps from a Compose file, a repository URL or the one-click catalog.

Click Web console and you’re logged in as root in a browser tab, with Hostinger Agent in a panel beside it. You don’t need an SSH client or keys. The login banner flagged 33 pending updates, 23 of them security fixes; more on that later. A quick docker --version confirmed Docker 29.9.3 was ready.

Here’s the whole app: a tiny HTTP server with a health check, and a Dockerfile on Node.js 24, a supported LTS line.

The build took 8.3 seconds, including pulling node:24-alpine, and the health check answered straight away. The --restart unless-stopped flag brings the container back after a crash or reboot, which I tested later.

That’s a working app, but on plain HTTP, on a high port, with no TLS. Getting it production-ready is the DevOps part I handed to the AI.

An AI-managed VPS isn’t the same as a traditional managed VPS. You still get a self-managed server with full root access, but Hostinger Agent does the work you’d normally do yourself. Agent (previously called Kodee) is an MCP-powered assistant that lives in the dashboard and beside the AI web terminal, and it’s included at no extra cost. You can also connect Claude or Cursor to your server through Hostinger’s MCP server.

My prompt: “Check that Docker is installed, then set up a reverse proxy with a free SSL certificate so my demo-app container (port 3000) is served at https://srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud.”

It started by looking around: the Docker and Compose versions, which ports were in use, whether a web server was already running, and the OS release. Then it installed Caddy from Caddy’s official repository, configured the proxy and made sure ufw allowed web traffic.

It read Caddy’s logs to confirm the Let’s Encrypt certificate, which arrived in about 6 seconds. Finally it tested its own work: HTTPS returned 200, and plain HTTP returned a 308 redirect. The whole task took about two minutes.

To give it a real fault, I ran docker stop demo-app and reloaded the site. Then I asked why it was returning a 502 and to fix it.

In a single command, it checked the container state (Exited (137)) and searched Caddy’s logs, where it found connect: connection refused on port 3000. It restarted the container, confirmed /health and HTTPS were back, and even pointed to my docker stop in the terminal history. It also explained that --restart unless-stopped covers crashes and reboots, but not a manual stop.

A tip it didn’t mention: exit code 137 means Docker had to force-kill the container, because Node running as PID 1 ignores SIGTERM. Add --init to docker run for clean, instant shutdowns.

This was the most impressive answer. ufw already allowed only those three ports, but Agent noticed port 3000 was still reachable from the internet, because Docker-published ports bypass ufw rules.

It recreated the container bound to 127.0.0.1:3000, so the app is only reachable through Caddy, and confirmed with an external port check. It left DNS, time sync and Caddy’s local admin port running, explaining that stopping them would break DNS and time sync.

Then I asked it to reboot the server. First it checked that Docker and Caddy start on boot and that the container’s restart policy was set, then it rebooted. The site came back over HTTPS without any help from me.

Nothing I asked for was destructive, so Agent just got on with it: the firewall change, the container rebuild and the reboot all ran without a confirmation prompt.

The Agent panel says so upfront, noting that terminal commands can run without one. For destructive actions such as reinstalling the OS or restoring a backup, Hostinger’s FAQ says Agent always asks for confirmation first.

One thing worth knowing: to close port 3000, Agent removed and recreated my container. That was harmless for a stateless demo, but a container that keeps data without a volume would lose it. Take a snapshot before big jobs, and keep the panel in view.

Here are the four plans at their Black Friday rates for a 24-month term.

For scale, here’s what my test used. With the Node app, Docker and Caddy all running, KVM 1 had 3.3 GB of its 4 GB RAM still available, and the app container itself used about 65 MB.

So 4 GB is plenty for one or two small apps. Move to 8 GB on KVM 2 once you add Coolify, a database, or n8n with its own Postgres, because those are the services that eat memory.

Finding a cheap VPS got harder in 2026, because memory got expensive. AI data centres are buying up DRAM, and hosts are passing the cost on. In April 2026, Hetzner raised cloud server prices by up to 37%, citing hardware costs, then raised prices again in June. On the same April date, OVHcloud’s entry VPS went from $4.90 to $7.60, a 55% jump. Nobody can promise where VPS prices go next, but the trend this year has been up.

This is how Hostinger’s Black Friday pricing works. You pay upfront for 12 or 24 months, and the 24-month term gives the lowest monthly rate. Your price is fixed for that whole term. After that, it renews at the standard rate.

On KVM 2, for example, you pay $210.96 today (the $8.79/mo rate) for 24 months, and it renews at $14.99/mo after that. Do the maths on the renewal before you commit, not just the headline rate.

Black Friday VPS deal: up to 70% off VPS hosting

Get up to 70% off Hostinger VPS plans this Black Friday, with prices from $6.49/mo on a 24-month term. Every plan includes Hostinger Agent, Docker Manager and free weekly backups. Your price is fixed for the term, then renews at the standard rate. The deal ends December 14, 2026.

#10477. VAST says petabyte SSDs will outgrow today’s parallel file system RAID

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Built from 15 TB drives, VAST’s 900 PB system would draw about 5.6 MW instead of 400 kW.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

VAST Data says petabyte SSDs will outgrow RAID in today’s parallel file systems, according to a simulation of its own 900 PB system that puts the odds of data loss at 1 in 11 over 10 years. At Supercomputing 2026 (SC26), the company explains how.

Bigger drives take longer to rebuild after a failure, leaving more time for further failures to lose data, but operators will still want those large drives because many sites are power-constrained and storage power scales with bandwidth rather than capacity. VAST’s recommended fix: system-wide erasure coding with at least three parity strips.

The company’s principal technical strategist, Glenn Lockwood, walked through the simulation, described as a “discrete event simulation” using real failure data, in an Exhibitor Forum talk during SC26’s HPC System Hardware and Architecture session. With petabyte-scale SSDs swapped in, enclosure-local declustered RAID — the approach used by DDN EXAScaler and HPE Cray ClusterStor, per Lockwood — has those exceptionally high odds of data loss. With global declustering and extra parity, operators can get this down to 1 in 54 million, a significant improvement, but VAST’s own scheme gets to about 1 in 62 billion, three orders of magnitude better.

Lockwood’s case for petabyte SSDs rests on how storage power scales: at about 28 W per SSD, regardless of capacity and including its share of the surrounding infrastructure, plus 20 W per GB/s of bandwidth. Getting 900 PB of flash from 15 TB SSDs would take more than 63K drives, each adding its own bandwidth, for 190 TB/s at 5.6 MW, or “the equivalent of 3,100 or so of Blackwell Ultra GPUs.” Lockwood says, though, that “real AI applications in our experience have never approached needing anything close to that level of performance” from the storage.

With 122 TB drives, at almost an 8K count and much lower 8 TB/s of aggregate bandwidth, the power draw is less than a tenth of that, at about 400 kW. Within that budget, 15 TB SSDs top out at about 250 PB, even with no power left for performance. That power could be better spent on compute and, anyway, 245 TB-class drives are already arriving, with much larger drives expected.

Everything is a series of trade-offs, though, and larger drives come with their own caveats. Lockwood acknowledges the usual reaction to a 245 TB drive: that it’s “a big blast radius,” since a single failure leaves much more to rebuild. And it does take longer: at the same per-drive speed, a 122 TB drive takes twice as long to rebuild as a 60 TB drive, leaving a longer window in which a second failure in a traditional RAID 5 set loses data. Per-drive capacity is potentially outpacing bandwidth increases and, at least within a generation, “the performance of each individual drive does not change,” so this is something that must be accepted or overcome.

The simulated system involves 7,920 SSDs at 122 TB each, using 180 enclosures with 44 drives each. With a RAID 5 single parity solution, there is a 100% chance of data loss over 10 years, but “no one uses single parity anymore.” Double parity still has 1 in 150 odds of data loss over a decade and is considered “state of the art for many open source file systems,” and he explicitly names Lustre and BeeGFS within this context.

For proprietary parallel file system implementations, declustered RAID within an enclosure is the state of the art, instead. Declustered RAID spreads each drive group’s data, parity, and spare space over more drives than usual, allowing for faster rebuilds. Keeping it within an enclosure means that the spread still stays within a single box of drives. Swapping petabyte SSDs into that enclosure-level declustering is what gets to the headline 1 in 11 odds of data loss.

As for the mentioned rivals, DDN’s DCR, in one published configuration, uses RAID 6 in an 8+2 array for a 24-NVMe SSD pool. That means eight data strips plus two parity strips per stripe, allowing any two drives in the pool to fail without losing data, and each stripe is spread over 10 of the 24 drives. DDN has conceded in the past that larger systems may need more than DCR. HPE’s declustered RAID solution is known as GridRAID, with declustered parity per enclosure half, meaning two 12-drive pools per 24-drive enclosure.

Perlmutter, the supercomputer at Berkeley Lab’s National Energy Research Scientific Computing Center, where Lockwood once worked, used GridRAID with 8+2+1 RAID 6 per 12-drive object storage target, Lustre’s name for a storage unit. The +1 is one spare drive’s worth of space, spread across all 12, so a rebuild writes to every surviving drive rather than to a single replacement. Either way, protection stops at two failures in a pool of one or two dozen drives, and only those drives can assist with a rebuild. This is the scenario in VAST’s simulation with the catastrophic failure rate.

Declustered RAID across the whole system, plus extra parity

Several solutions exist, with limits. Adding a parity strip for triple parity reduces the odds of data loss. Global declustering, on its own, can also reduce the odds a little bit, with faster rebuild times. However, stripes are spread over every drive in the cluster, so two random failures are more likely to hit the same stripe, meaning a larger fault domain.

If both approaches are used, which is the minimum bar for him, you get down to getting struck by “lightning on your birthday” odds. VAST already publishes quadruple parity, which is beyond this bar. Using VAST’s own “special magic,” undisclosed but beyond the minimum bar, drops the odds even further, specifically for the petabyte SSDs, which is the real target of the talk.

Then we get to the metadata, the data about data, which in this case is the index of where every piece of every file dwells. Every write necessarily updates it synchronously, so VAST keeps it on separate, fast SSDs as three full copies for replication, not parity. This means it can survive two failures, which is less than the data’s four parity strips, and the index becomes the weakest link. With 122 TB drives, he puts the data drives’ odds at 1 in 78 trillion over 10 years, against 1 in 58 million for metadata. To Lockwood, this illustrates that the surrounding infrastructure is the real risk, given the failure gap between the index and regular data.

Rebuilding in the index area starts about 12 seconds after a failure, on average, versus about two minutes for data drives, Lockwood says. VAST’s procedure more generally, for when a drive drops out, is to power-cycle it and hope, so there is always some delay before a rebuild is forced. This slight delay barely matters for data drives with four parity strips but is more impactful for the three-copy metadata index drives. For example, waiting an hour for data drives “wouldn’t make a big difference” while just 20 minutes or more on a metadata drive has “extremely high” risk.

Earlier this year, in May, Micron began shipping the 245 TB 6600 ION, which pulls up to 30 W at maximum load. Kioxia has the LC9, used in a Dell 2U server with 40 drives for a total of 9.8 PB. SanDisk also has its 256 TB UltraQLC, which was due in the first half of this year, and Solidigm is targeting about 245 TB later this year.

Meanwhile, multiple roadmaps reach petabyte-class drives, including SanDisk’s UltraQLC, a target by SK hynix for QLC SSDs, and Samsung at some time in the future. This strengthens VAST’s case and, while at least the power math adds up, data still needs to be compared openly and fairly to make a firm conclusion about the best pathway forward for what is clearly an unavoidable problem.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

#10475. Expect Samsung’s good-value Galaxy devices to get more expensive, report says

Rising memory costs are hitting mid-range devices the hardest. The latest report suggests Samsung will increase the price of its Galaxy A device series by 19% next year.

The report comes from Korean news outlet SEDaily. An industry source has reportedly stated that Samsung plans to increase the price of its mid-range phones starting next year. More specifically, the Galaxy A series.

The range between price jumps varies across a reported 8 Galaxy A series devices, but the highest value difference nearly hits a notable 19% increase. The largest jump reaches 18.6% for the Galaxy A17, which could increase from $269 to $319. It would no longer sit in a sub-$300 spot and wouldn’t be much of a value buy anymore.

Again, these are phones praised for sitting at a convenient price point. Samsung’s Galaxy A devices are generally good value and offer a lot of the best One UI features for a price that doesn’t come close to the current flagship selling points.

This comes after a report came out last month indicating Samsung cut production of its Galaxy A devices by “approximately 20%.” Memory makes up more of the overall component cost in mid-range devices. So the cost of the overall device is more influenced by memory prices than in a flagship, like the Galaxy S26 Ultra. Even with a specced-out build, there’s more dispersion of costly components, rather than one expensive component like memory taking up the bulk of the production price.

That cut is estimated to be around 10 million units, and this latest report only furthers the claim that mid-range devices are too costly to make for Samsung.

The opposite is true for the Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, which reportedly got the green light for increased production prior to launch. Price suggestions average an increase of $200 for the Galaxy S27 and Galaxy S27 Plus. As for the high-spec Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, they start at $1,399 and $1,499, respectively.

There was already a massive disparity between those devices and the increasingly validated Galaxy A devices, and the gap is shrinking.

#10473. Anthropic mathematician claims to have solved 167-year-old math problem with Claude

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In the same week that Anthropic was floated on the Nasdaq in its initial public offering, a mathematician employed by the company claims to have discovered proof behind one of the world's most famous math problem, the 167-year-old Riemann hypothesis. Levent Alpöge wrote in a paper this week, "I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis."

As you're no doubt already aware, and as Alpöge explains, the Riemann Hypothesis is the classic problem in number theory and one of the most famous unsolved problems in mathematics. Proposed in 1859 by Bernhard Riemann, who gave the problem its name, it is the theory that prime numbers are not in fact random but determined. "The Riemann hypothesis asserts that all interesting solutions of the equation ζ(s) = 0 lie on a certain vertical straight line," the Clay Mathematics Institute explains. "This has been checked for the first 10,000,000,000,000 solutions. A proof that it is true for every interesting solution would shed light on many of the mysteries surrounding the distribution of prime numbers."

As Scientific American notes, the problem is the "North Star for the entire field of number theory, and the most famous open problem in math." Furthermore, the problem was previously thought so insurmountable that experts didn't even bother working on it.

Alpöge not only believes that he has used Claude to discover a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis, but also believes the method could easily extend to the so-called Generalized Riemann Hypothesis and the Extended Riemann Hypothesis, too. "An astounding amount of mathematics is present in the argument, far more than I had imagined would be necessary as a student," he writes.

This would not be the first time that AI has made a significant discovery or finding in a major STEM field. Earlier this year, OpenAI helped create a breakthrough solution for the Navier-Stokes problem, a finding somewhat overshadowed by a plagiarism controversy.

Alpöge's statement is not without reservation. Aside from confirming whether Claude has actually helped crack the Riemann Hypothesis, which will take some time, he admits sadness at the finding. "It is natural to feel that this is an end, in my case a loss of a principal guiding light, but what I saw during the course of progress so amazed me that I believe it is one dawn across science of something far greater."

"In a time of revolution in mathematics, I hope the argument provides a glimpse towards the miracles that lie ahead," Alpöge adds.

#10469. Is Kling 4.0 the Right AI Video Generator for Your Project?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A short clip can help a project in different ways. It can show an idea you have not filmed, put an existing design in motion, or give an announcement something to look at. The same approach will not work if the clip needs to demonstrate a real feature or show exactly what happened. Before choosing a generator, decide what the viewer needs to learn from the shot.

This article uses Kling 4.0 as a worked example, but the decision process applies to any AI video generator. It covers how these tools turn prompts and images into video, how Kling 4.0 and a few alternatives differ, how to choose a starting mode, how to add exact text and branding outside the generated clip with a short ffmpeg script, and when real footage is the better choice. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

● Generated video is best for imagined shots. It suits concepts, backgrounds and early explorations, not instructions or faithful records.

● Your starting material decides the mode. A written idea, a reference image or a pair of opening and closing frames each call for a different input.

● Keep exact wording out of the generated clip. Titles, dates and logos belong on separate layers in an editor.

● Tools differ on control, cost and licensing. Compare inputs, length, resolution, credits and terms before you commit.

● Always review. Watch the whole clip at its final size and check it against the real product or design.

Most current systems extend diffusion models across time. A model learns to turn noise into a coherent set of frames guided by a prompt, and sometimes by an image or a clip. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequences are easy to observe.

● The prompt describes change over time. One visible change and one camera instruction usually work better than a long list of actions.

● Reference material steers but does not lock. A picture can suggest shape and colour without guaranteeing an exact match.

● Longer clips give more room for drift. Faces, logos and fine text are the first things to change between frames.

● Cost grows with length and resolution. Most hosted tools charge credits per second, so short previews are cheaper than full shots.

If you only have an idea, start with text. For a short announcement, you might ask for a band of light to move across a wall of coloured tiles. No one needs to recognise a particular wall, so the model can decide how it looks. Give it one visible change and a clear view of the subject. A prompt such as Soft morning light travels slowly across a wall of blue ceramic tiles. Fixed camera, no lettering is enough to see whether the idea works.

If you already have an illustration or a photo, reference mode gives you another starting point. Suppose you want a short concept clip for a desk accessory that only exists as a drawing. The picture can show the general shape and colour while the prompt describes the view you want. A video reference can help when it is the motion that is hard to explain, such as a slow camera slide. Say what each asset is meant to guide, and do not add a clip just because you have one.

Use keyframes when a particular view matters at a particular moment. Start with an opening image. If a short reveal also needs to end with the object facing forward, add an ending image to show that view. That is different from a reference image that mainly shows what the object looks like. Neither method guarantees an exact match, so check the result rather than assuming the uploaded picture has locked the design.

No single tool fits every project. The table summarizes what each option’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

Reading the table: hosted studios such as the one on JXP are the quickest way to test an idea and need no setup, but you pay per second and work within their supported inputs. Kling’s own site is the place to check announcements and API options directly, since its page says little about durations or resolution. Runway positions its models for motion quality and prompt following, while Google’s Gemini API video documentation suits teams building generation into their own products. The Diffusers video guide covers open-source pipelines for developers who want local control and are ready to manage GPUs and licences.

Trying an idea before making it. An illustrator might want to see how an imagined landscape feels when the light changes. A quick clip gives the team something to discuss: is the change visible, and does it happen at the right pace? It does not need to be the final animation to answer those questions.

Showing a product concept. A rotating view or a slow camera move can help people discuss how a design might be presented. It is useful early, when the shape and lighting are still being explored. Once the clip represents a product someone can buy, the standard changes. If a port moves or a button appears that the real product does not have, it is the wrong clip for that job.

Making a short visual for a post. A few seconds of moving colour can give an event announcement or a project update a distinctive opening. Put the name, date and other exact wording in your editor so they are readable and easy to change. The background only needs to support the message. If it competes with the text, use less movement or keep the background still.

Generated video is unreliable at lettering, so add titles in an editor or with a script. The command below overlays a title on a generated background and then checks the file properties. It assumes you already have a clip named background.mp4 and ffmpeg installed.

-vf "drawtext=text='Launch Night | 12 March':fontcolor=white:fontsize=64:x=(w-text_w)/2:y=(h-text_h)/2:box=1:boxcolor=black@0.35:boxborderw=24" \

-c:v libx264 -pix_fmt yuv420p -an announcement.mp4

-show_entries stream=width,height,r_frame_rate,duration \

-of default=noprint_wrappers=1 announcement.mp4

Because the text lives in the command, you can reuse the same background for three events by changing one string. The font may need to be set with a fontfile option on some systems. This is the practical version of keeping the moving background in one file and the editable details in another.

If people will follow the clip as instructions, use real footage or a screen recording for the steps. A convincing shot of an app is not proof that a button is in the right place. A generated close-up of a product is not a reliable way to show how its parts fit. You can still use an imagined opening, but make the switch to the real demonstration clear.

Think about revisions, too. If your team expects to adjust the same title, logo or date every week, put those elements on separate layers in an editor. And if the only movement you need is a slow zoom on an approved picture, apply that zoom directly. There is no reason to risk changing the picture when a simple edit will do.

This matters when someone else will maintain the video. A reusable announcement template is easier to hand over if the moving background is one file and the event details are editable. If the team likes the movement but needs three versions with different dates, they can reuse the same clip. If every change calls for a new generation, the time you saved on the first version may disappear on the second.

The Kling 4.0 video generator on JXP currently offers 720p and 1080p settings and clip lengths from 3 to 30 seconds. According to its page, reference mode accepts up to 10 images and 5 videos, keyframe mode takes a first frame, middle frames and an optional last frame, and credits are charged per second with a higher rate at 1080p. For a first attempt, choose the frame shape you plan to use and make the clip only as long as it needs to be. Check the displayed credit cost before generating, because a short preview can tell you whether the direction works before you spend on a longer shot.

Decide what a good result looks like before you generate it. For the tile background, you need to see the light move without distracting from the words you will add. For a product concept, check whether the shape stays recognisable as the viewpoint changes. Watch the whole clip at the size the audience will see it. If the shot misses the point, revise one thing at a time: the motion, the framing or the source image.

● Read the licence. JXP’s page lists a commercial use license on each plan and says outputs are for creative and lawful use, with the user responsible for reviewing them. Other tools have different terms.

● Use inputs you have the right to use. Do not upload other people’s copyrighted work or photos of people without permission.

● Do not misrepresent products or events. A generated clip should not suggest features or footage that do not exist.

● Consider provenance. C2PA is an open standard for Content Credentials that records where content came from and how it was edited, and some tools support it.

● Disclose where required. Follow platform and advertising rules on labelling AI-generated or altered content.

● Fine text, logos, hands and fast motion can drift or flicker between frames.

● Reference images and keyframes steer the result but do not guarantee an exact match.

● Vendor specifications, such as resolution and length, can differ between a model announcement and a particular platform.

● Credit costs and plan terms change, so check them on the day you generate.

● Long or exact sequences are often cheaper and safer to film than to generate.

Short imagined shots, concept clips and moving backgrounds, where some details can be left open and the clip is reviewed before use.

Use text when you only have an idea, reference mode when you have a picture or clip to guide the look, and keyframes when specific views matter at specific moments.

Not reliably. Use real footage or a screen recording for instructions and demonstrations.

No. Hosted studios, vendor sites, developer APIs and open-source pipelines all exist. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your project.

Kling 4.0 is a good candidate when the missing piece is an imagined shot and you can describe what it needs to do. Pick one such shot, try it, and put it next to the material you already have, and compare at least one alternative before you commit. If the project needs exact instructions or a faithful record, use the real thing. A useful first result is one that does its job in the edit, not simply one that looks impressive on its own.

#10468. The two best kids’ smartwatches are $50-$64 off ahead of Black Friday

Garmin and Bark are competing for your money this year, and these sales make it easy to get something fun that’ll keep your kids safe and in touch.

Many parents struggle with the idea of getting a smartphone for their kids. If you want your kids to stay in touch but not have access to all the garbage on the internet, a smartwatch is the best alternative. I’ve tested a lot of kids’ smartwatches over the years, and I can easily recommend the two in the picture above: the Bark Watch and the Garmin Bounce 2.

Both watches are on sale well ahead of Black Friday, too! The Bark Watch is $11 off at Amazon ($274 total) and $50 off at Bark, while the Garmin Bounce 2 is $50-$64 off (starting at $235) depending on the color you choose. The least expensive Garmin Bounce 2 is the blue color in the photo I took above, which my son loved.

Choosing between the two is easy enough. Garmin’s watch is the least complicated and comes in a slightly more durable case with fun colors. It’s slightly cheaper than Bark, but doesn’t have any health sensors on board. It mainly tracks steps, allows your kids to message or call you, and gives you geofencing and location tracking through a $9.99 per month LTE subscription plan. It’s got a 5ATM rating and a polycarbonate case, so it’s quite durable.

The Garmin Bounce 2 is preloaded with a few mini games and educational apps, plus the Garmin Jr. app has built-in unlockables and rewards parents can set for doing chores, completing schoolwork, etc. Battery life is roughly 2 days with normal use, but can be less if you’re in an area with poor LTE reception. The Garmin Bounce 2 is best for kids 10 and under.

The Bark Watch uses Samsung Galaxy Watch 8 hardware and a custom version of Wear OS. It’s a much more powerful watch and only gets a day of battery life because of it. This one feels like a midpoint between a kids’ watch and a standard Wear OS watch, as it has all the power of Wear OS but doesn’t feature the Google Play Store. It doesn’t have any games, social media, or browsers.

Since it uses Samsung’s hardware, it includes cutting-edge health sensors that your kids can use, plus great multitasking and a svelte, attractive design. Bark charges $15 per month for its plan, which includes a Bark App subscription for the whole family. I also use the Bark App on my son’s phone, which helps me monitor his usage and make sure he’s safe online. The extra $5 per month adds up, but it’s absolutely worth it if you want to add the Bark App to another phone or tablet.

The Bark Watch is fine enough for younger kids, but the design and lack of games feel more like an “older kids’ watch” to me, and my 12-year-old son agrees. Either way you go, these are fantastic choices that you’ll be happy with, especially with a nice little deal.

The top scorer of South Korea's Certified Tax Accountant exam confessed to AI-assisted cheating, prompting the South Korean government to tighten anti-cheating measures for its incredibly important CSAT exams.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

South Korea used emergency disaster alerts during its national college admission test to catch prohibited devices. The anti-cheating measure was announced after a cheater in South Korea’s tax accountant exam confessed to using AI and a micro-camera to cheat. The alerts caught a total of 54 candidates, with 111 total when combined with other means, The Korean Herald reports.

The College Scholastic Ability Test (CSAT), also known as Suneung, is one of South Korea’s most important yearly events. The exam is so significant that the whole country pauses and makes way for the test-takers; police clear the roads and escort late students, noisy construction and maintenance are suspended, aircraft are grounded, military exercises are paused, and the stock market opens an hour later. The government even requests public institutions and private companies start work later, delaying the rush hour and ensuring the students arrive unimpeded.

The brutal exam, held every third Thursday of November, lasts eight hours, from 8:40 AM to 5:45 PM, often being the deciding factor for the trajectory of the students’ lives. With such high stakes, cheating is not unusual.

The month before the CSAT, South Korea’s Certified Tax Accountant exam received an unusually large number of high scorers — with 66 candidates scoring over 90+ in introductory accounting over only two in 2025 and one in 2024, as reported by The Korean Herald. The top scorer, Han Won-seok, confessed to cheating on his lawyer’s YouTube channel, admitting to cheating using a micro-camera that feeds images of the test to an AI on his smartphone.

The confession went live days before the Suneung, prompting the South Korean government to tighten up its anti-cheating measures for the exam, with Prime Minister Lee Jae Myung stating, "Cheating involving the abuse of AI in South Korea, where public examinations significantly affect the distribution of opportunities and resources, rocks the basic order of our society by undermining fairness and trust," calling for an overhaul of the system to address AI-assisted cheating, as reported by Yonhap News Agency.

The first alert rang at 8:30 AM, ten minutes before the exam started, catching 31 students with unauthorized devices, whose scores were immediately invalidated despite the test not having started yet. The next two alerts at 2:21 and 2:57 PM caught 23 more, bringing the total to 54. As of today, Yonhap News Agency reports that 111 total students were caught with unauthorized devices — 52 were caught through other methods such as metal detectors, WiFi signal detection, and physical inspections, with a further five caught red-handed using prohibited devices.

Alongside the South Korean government’s reaction to the confession, a law school entrance exam candidate accused of AI-assisted cheating like Han Won-seok had died on the day of the Suneung. In July, Lee Jin-woo, a Legal Education Eligibility Test (LEET) candidate, achieved an impressive perfect score and is reportedly the first to do so. Months later, he published a book about his study methods, talking about lifestyle changes, exercise, diet, and even reduced smartphone use.

#10463. Hands-on: Sony’s Walkman-revival USB-C headphones are back in stock and worth a look

Sony’s iconic Walkman headphones are back in the USB-C MDR-3R and, after quickly selling out on launch day, they’ve now been restocked. And, after using them, I can see why they sold out so quickly.

The MDR-3R are Sony’s revival of the wired over-ear headphones that many used with a classic Walkman player. The retro style still looks great in 2026, but using an original pair is a bit of a headache in today’s age of headphone jack-less smartphones. So it was nice to see Sony give this a revival with USB-C.

While the style won’t be for everyone, I think these work brilliantly. The sound quality is excellent and having a pair of headphones that doesn’t fully enclose my ears is pretty refreshing. There’s no noise cancellation, but with open-ear earbuds becoming more popular, I actually really like these as a similar solution (though high volumes will definitely be heard by those around you). I’m not fully cut off from the world around me, but I’m also able to get good sound quality. Lows are the weakness here, with unimpressive bass drops, but the sound quality is crisp and clear and pretty neutral, and the open-ish style makes it feel like the room around me is filled with music.

The physical hardware is great. While a plastic and metal build is objectively not as comfy as a premium pair of ANC headphones, the extremely light weight helps MDR-3R become an afterthought. You simply forget you’re wearing them after a few minutes.

The earpads are swappable, with orange and blue options included in the box.

As for the USB-C cable, it’s fully plug-and-play. No app needed and it worked across every device I tested (including Pixel, which doesn’t work with all USB-C audio output). I do wish the cable was perhaps a few inches longer, but it’s long enough to wear the headphones with my phone in my front pocket. There’s a remote along the cable for play/pause and volume adjustments, though I think it’s a little too high on the cable.

For $59.99, these are a fun pair of USB-C headphones to have on hand. They sound good, work great, and don’t cost an arm and a leg. I can see why they sold out so quickly on launch day – in just 10 hours, Sony told me.

Sony MDR-3R are back in stock now and shipping in early December. If you want a pair, I say don’t wait. They’re well worth at least trying if you can get them for retail price – please don’t give in to the scalpers selling these at double, they’re not worth that.

#10461. Clicks Communicator hits a supply chain snag, and it isn’t because of RAM

Clicks is still on track to release the phone with a physical keyboard within the next few months, but a Communicator launch before the end of this year isn’t looking very likely as shipping gets pushed back.

The Communicator is a fresh take on what an Android phone can be. It was first introduced at CES earlier this year, with the main selling angle being the physically integrated keyboard. Clicks wants to target those who are looking for a second device, and the Communicator fits the bill.

Last we heard, the Clicks Communicator was on track to launch and ship in December, following a cheery update detailing an upgrade to 12GB of RAM and other changes. However, Clicks posted a new update today stating that the timeline has shifted out a month because of supply chain issues caused by something other than RAM.

Unfortunately, we’ve encountered a delay with securing a critical supply chain component needed to fabricate our printed circuit board ahead of manufacturing and final assembly. Assembly will now begin in early January, with deliveries expected to start in late January.

It notes that the “critical supply chain component” is the essential fabric used for the company’s printed circuit boards. The blog post dives into some neat details about the component holding things up. The PCBs Clicks is using are made up of more material layers than some competitor boards. But the fabric used to layer those boards is in short supply at this sort of demand, with four manufacturers reportedly unable to meet the company’s required volume.

The post also spells out difficulties caused by troubles in the Middle East, and rising memory costs straining the chain of supply further, even if memory – surprisingly – isn’t the cause of this small delay. Those compounded issues have pushed production back, and users are now told to expect their Clicks Communicator pre-orders to be shipped in late January, at the earliest. Many pre-orders will likely be shipped after that because of further time constraints before Chinese New Year.

In an act of good faith, Clicks says it’s including a set of 3.5mm wired headphones in each box for orders placed prior to today.

A push from December to January isn’t massive, and Clicks still seems to be making solid time on developing a form factor we don’t often see. You can still pre-order the Clicks Communicator starting at $649.
