#10460. How Consumer AI Companions Handle Context Windows, Persistent Memory, and Voice Latency

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI companions are the most demanding consumer use of large language models right now. A coding assistant can forget your last message and still be useful. A companion that forgets your name, your running story, or the plan you made last Tuesday has failed at its one job. On top of that, users now expect voice calls that feel like a phone conversation, not a walkie-talkie exchange with a five-second pause.

Three engineering problems decide whether a companion app works: how much of the conversation fits in the model's context window, what the app remembers once the window fills up, and how fast the voice pipeline answers. Here is how consumer companions handle each one, with real tier and pricing examples, and what developers can take from their designs for their own products.

Most LLM products are judged on single answers. Companions are judged on continuity. That creates pressure in three places at once:

Every consumer companion is a different set of trade-offs across those three constraints, and the trade-offs are increasingly how the apps are priced.

The context window is the total number of tokens the model can see when it writes the next reply. In a companion app, that budget is spent before the user says anything:

When the chat log outgrows what is left, the oldest messages fall out. The user experiences this as the companion "forgetting" mid-conversation. This is why context size is now a headline spec in the category, and a paid feature. SpicyChat AI, for example, gives free accounts a 4K context window, then scales memory to 8K on its $19.95/mo tier and 32K on its $29.95/mo tier. Janitor AI runs about 9K of context on its own JanitorLLM. Independent uncensored AI chat benchmarks record these context sizes alongside refusal behavior and pricing, because context length predicts how long a roleplay stays coherent better than any marketing claim.

Some apps sell memory in messages rather than tokens. Fantasy GF's pricing page lists a 60-message base "smart memory" and 5x that, 300 messages, on its Ultra plan. Others sell it as a memory multiplier on higher subscription tiers. The pattern is consistent across the category: memory depth is the upgrade lever, not the model itself.

No consumer app keeps an entire relationship in the prompt. Persistent memory is a separate system that writes to storage and reads back into the context window on each turn. In practice it has three layers:

The differences users notice are usually retention policies, not algorithms. Crushon AI's own subscription docs say free-plan chat history is deleted after 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans keep it for the membership period. Paid tiers across the category commonly promise that memories never expire. There is also a security angle worth stating plainly: companion chat logs are among the most sensitive data a consumer product can hold. A 2024 breach at Muah.ai, first reported by 404 Media, exposed roughly 1.9 million users' email addresses together with their private prompts. Any app that builds persistent memory is also building a liability, and developers copying the pattern should treat the memory store as the most protected part of the system.

A voice call in a companion app is three models chained together: speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech. Run them strictly in sequence and a single reply can take several seconds, which reads as dead air. The apps that feel fast attack each stage the same way:

With every stage streaming, time-to-first-audio can land close to a second on a good connection, which is roughly the threshold where a conversation stops feeling laggy. Voice is also expensive to run - three model calls instead of one, plus audio bandwidth - which is why it is metered harder than text. Fantasy GF charges 8 tokens per minute for voice calls (4 on Ultra), which turns the 100 monthly tokens on its entry plan into about 12 minutes of call time. SpicyChat, for its part, only adds text-to-speech at its top tier. Latency engineering and unit economics are the same problem here: faster pipelines cost less per call, and cheaper calls are what make voice viable below the top plan.

Tier details below come from each app's own pricing or documentation pages, checked November 16, 2026, and cross-checked against il.ly's AI companion testing guide, which hands-tests 15 companion apps across memory, media, and voice.

Two takeaways from the table. First, nobody sells a bigger base model - they sell a bigger window into the same one. Second, the free tiers are deliberately memory-poor: limited context and short retention are the upgrade prompt.

Even if you never build a companion, these apps are a useful reference architecture for any stateful LLM product:

Consumer AI companions have turned three hard infrastructure problems - bounded context, durable memory, and real-time voice - into their pricing pages. Context size, memory depth, and voice access are the tiers. If you are building anything stateful on an LLM, this category is worth watching closely: it is solving continuity and latency problems at consumer scale, in public, one subscription tier at a time.

It is the total amount of text, measured in tokens, that the model can consider when writing its next reply. The character definition, stored memories, recent chat, and the new message all share that budget. When the conversation exceeds it, the oldest content drops out and the companion appears to forget.

Yes, through a memory system separate from the context window. Apps summarize older chats, extract stable facts about you into a profile, and retrieve relevant past moments into the prompt. Retention varies: some free tiers delete chat history after as little as 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans typically keep memory for the membership period.

A voice turn runs three models - speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech - instead of one, plus audio transfer. Providers pass that cost on through per-minute token charges or by locking voice behind higher tiers.

For casual chat, 4K to 9K tokens works. For long-running roleplay or a companion expected to hold plot threads for weeks, 32K and up, or a well-built memory layer on top of a smaller window, makes the visible difference.

#10459. TPU Model Design Considerations and Optimization Guide

Google designed TPUs from the ground up to be exceptionally efficient for machine learning workloads, delivering top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Because TPUs trade general-purpose hardware scheduling for wide systolic arrays, dedicated SparseCore engines, and custom interconnect fabrics, adhering to specific architectural best practices is essential to achieve maximum performance. To help developers get the most out of TPUs, we are launching the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign guide. This post highlights its key principles—spanning shape alignment, architectural choices like LatentMoE, and parallelism strategies—to accelerate LLMs, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, and other hardware-intensive workloads on TPUs.

Performance tuning starts with model definition. Architectural choices made before the first training step set permanent ceilings on MFU (Model FLOP Utilization), cost, and serving latency. This guide gives concrete, hardware-grounded design rules for teams building or adapting models for TPUs, organized into three groups:

How to use this guide. The shape rules apply to anyone designing a new model. If you are adapting an existing model rather than designing one, the Expressing Parallelism section applies without touching the checkpoint. The general rules hold for any large-matmul architecture (dense LLMs, multimodal, and world models), and the companion AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository grows model- and workload-specific sections over time.

Eighth-generation TPUs. These principles carry forward to TPU 8t and TPU 8i and to the newly announced TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max. The reasoning stays the same and the thresholds move with the hardware. TPU 8t and TPU 8i both add native FP4. For training, TPU 8t also brings more balanced Vector Processing Unit (VPU) scaling. For serving, TPU 8i adds the Boardfly topology, a Collectives Acceleration Engine (CAE), and 3x more on-chip SRAM. We note the effects inline in Principles 5–8, and follow-up posts will cover eighth-generation designs in depth.

Measured evidence. Alongside the published sources, notes marked Measured carry results from our own Ironwood profiling program: open 20–30B MoEs (a Qwen3-30B-derived LatentMoE variant; GPT-OSS-20B) plus dense controls, single slice (except for the 512-chip TPU v7x and 1,024-chip TPU v4 ablations in Principle 4), BF16, training shapes up to 32K sequence, all against tuned-systems baselines. Treat the trends and rankings as transferable, the exact percentages as config-specific.

TPUs are purpose-built custom ML ASICs designed from the ground up to deliver state-of-the-art performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). While their architectural choices—such as wide 256×256 systolic arrays, software-managed VMEM, dedicated SparseCore engines, and direct multi-dimensional ICI fabrics—enable exceptional efficiency, they inherently create specific structural constraints and programming patterns that must be carefully followed to unlock maximal performance. Reaching peak efficiency requires aligning model shapes, routing, and parallelism directly with these underlying hardware characteristics:

Make d_model, FFN and expert intermediate widths (d_ff), latent widths, attention projections, and padded vocabulary sizes (used as contracting or output dimensions) multiples of 256 to match the 256×256 MXU systolic array. Token/batch axes and counts such as heads, layers, and experts are exempt. The most frequently missed dimension is head_dim: at 64 or 128, flash-attention QK products leave the MXU ≥ 50% idle. Reshape to head_dim = 256 with proportionally fewer Q and KV heads, preserving total projection widths and logical KV cache bytes (iso-quality in our ablations).

Measured: Reshaping Qwen3-30B MoE attention from 32 × 128 to 16 × 256 (identical parameters and FLOPs) sped up end-to-end training, with gains widening at longer context:

These rules apply for non-quadratic attention as well, notably GDN ablations from Yang et al. 2025 also noted at par or better quality for head dimension 256 for their Gated DeltaNet Attention.

XLA compiles each program for exact tensor shapes, so every buffer has a fixed address and every loop a known trip count, and a new shape means a recompilation and a host stall. What about dynamic shapes? Dynamic workloads fit this model: the trick is to separate the shape of a buffer from the extent of the data inside it.

Instead of padding to the largest group or the longest sequence, we keep the buffers at fixed shapes (tokens sorted by expert and padded only at the tail, the KV cache as a fixed pool of pages) and describe the ragged structure with a few small integer arrays (group sizes and offsets; KV lengths, the page table, cumulative query lengths). Pallas copies these into the TensorCore's scalar memory before the grid starts (PrefetchScalarGridSpec), and the kernel reads them to choose the next tile and bound its loops, with a mask covering a tile that straddles a group boundary or a partly filled last page. The data dependence becomes an address and a trip count instead of a shape, so the compiled program is the same for every routing and every batch composition and the kernel runs at the unpadded FLOP count.

Standard EP dispatches every routed token at full d_model width, making EP + FSDP slower than plain FSDP in our Ironwood benchmarks. LatentMoE projects routed tokens from d_model down to latent width ℓ (d/ℓ ≈ 2–4×) before dispatch, executes experts in latent space, and projects back up after combine (introduced by Nemotron 3 and adopted by 962B MAI-Base-1 and 2.8T Kimi K3):

Measured: On a 30B / 128-expert MoE, an ℓ = 768 (2.67×) LatentMoE cut on-device permute time 2.8×, lifted end-to-end training throughput +14% tokens/s (322 vs 270 TFLOP/s/chip, or +19%, since the latent projections add ~5% FLOPs per token; tokens stay ℓ-wide past the down-projection, so on-device permute and combine shrink alongside EP all-to-all), and freed ~25 GB/device of activation HBM when remat checkpoints were anchored on the compressed latent. While current MoE recipes in tpu-recipes run FSDP (+ TP), LatentMoE + FP8 dispatch makes EP roofline-viable when per-device batch is too small to hide FSDP weight all-gathers. Under both FSDP and EP, avoid over-fragmenting experts: small tokens × K / N group sizes starve MXU tiles.

When candidate MoE layouts match on quality, pick the one with higher compute intensity at a similar active-parameter budget. A larger shared expert (wider d_shared) shifts work into dense, communication-free GEMMs that overlap EP dispatch/combine in training and amortize weight reads across the decode batch. Fewer, wider routed experts (halving N and K while doubling d_ff at fixed sparsity K/N) cut dispatch traffic in half and double per-expert GEMM fill. Optimal (N, K) grows with model size, and over-coarsening hurts routing diversity. Ablate expert granularity and the shared-to-routed split at your target scale rather than copying GPU co-designed models, since the TPU optimum is more shared-heavy.

Measured: On a 63B LatentMoE (ℓ = 768, K/N = 1/32, 8K context, 512 TPU v7x chips at EP = 128), halving (N, K) from (512, 16) to (256, 8) while doubling d_ff and the shared expert lifted training throughput +57% tok/s and held lower loss across 120.8B tokens:

Low-concurrency decode streams model weights every token (~300 concurrent tokens per weight shard are needed in dense FP8 to reach compute roofline). Combine two architectural levers with explicit concurrency sizing:

Norms, activations, masking, routing math and quantization scale applies run on the Vector Processing Unit (VPU), which is roughly two orders of magnitude below the MXU in throughput and executes in sequence with the matmuls unless XLA fuses it into an adjacent GEMM or we move it off the TensorCore. Therefore we drop reshapes and transposes that carry no quality, standardize on fusable RMSNorm and SwiGLU so the elementwise work folds into the neighbouring GEMM, and offload the MoE sort, gather and scatter to the SparseCore.

Note that fused VPU work is free only while its cycle count fits inside the MXU window it is fused into. A quick illustration of this concept is block-scaled fp8 GEMM.

With a scale per block of B elements along the contraction axis, the MXU emits one partial sum per output element per block and the VPU rescales each one (about three vector ops), so per output element the MXU does 2K flops and the VPU does 3·ceil(K/B) ops. The rescale hides when B ≥ 1.5 × r_mxu / r_vpu, which on v7x (fp8 peak 4,614 TFLOPS against a 19.2 TFLOPS vector peak, a ratio near 240) puts the threshold near 340. In simulation on a DeepSeek-V3 expert shape, block 512 runs at the per-tensor time, block 256 is VPU-bound at 1.2x and block 128 at 2.4x. Achieved MXU efficiency is lower than peak, which lowers the effective ratio and can make the smaller blocks workable; the way to decide is to measure the GEMM at each block instead of using peak ratio. This threshold is specific to Ironwood. TPU 8t is designed with specialized support for scaling so quantization for MXFP4 and MXFP8 formats has no overhead. Re-measure the block-size threshold there instead of reusing 340. The rule itself carries forward: keep vector work hidden inside a matmul window.

For workloads dominated by BF16-sensitive matmuls (most notably long-context quadratic attention where direct FP8 degrades accuracy), native single-pass BF16 leaves MXU capacity unused whenever hardware low-precision MXU throughput exceeds native BF16 throughput by more than the pass count. Use the multi-pass BF16 algorithm to execute BF16-accurate matmuls via multiple passes over the higher-throughput lower-precision MXU pipeline without losing numerical fidelity. This matters more as low-precision throughput pulls ahead of BF16. TPU 8t adds native FP4, which doubles MXU throughput. On Ironwood the FP8 to BF16 ratio is exactly 2:1, so two or more passes cannot beat native BF16.

Assign communication-heavy parallel axes to the fastest physical links and keep logical mesh factors aligned with torus dimensions:

Minimum-degree CP matched to kernel trade-offs: All-gather CP is simplest (though its all-gathers can sit exposed) when paired with zigzag load balancing (context_parallel_load_balance in MaxText) so causal masking does not idle tail ranks; Ring CP overlaps collectives but trades off SplashAttention chunk efficiency; Ulysses A2A CP avoids causal skew but exposes all-to-alls and caps at head count.

Start every project with the quality-safe foundation: 256-aligned dimensions (1), static shapes with dropless routing (2), one parallelism plan mapped to physical links (8), wall-clock ablations on a tuned stack, and published reference targets (both in Measuring what matters below). For MoEs, add LatentMoE (3) after an ablation to cut routing/dispatch traffic and decode weight reads. Then prioritize by bottleneck regime:

Google engineered TPUs from the ground up as custom ML ASICs to deliver top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Unlocking that potential requires bridging the gap between hardware architecture and model design. By standardizing on 256-aligned shapes, leveraging LatentMoE, and mapping parallel axes directly to physical links, engineering teams can maximize TPU efficiency across both training and serving workloads.

A reference blueprint is only as good as the loop used to validate it. Always evaluate architectural changes in wall-clock time on a tuned software stack rather than paper FLOPs: in our Ironwood benchmarks, pure systems tuning (SparseCore offload, MXU-aligned tiles, and remat tuning) lifted an open 30B MoE from 12% to 23% MFU before any model change, and published Ironwood baselines show 256-aligned MoEs (DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3-235B at 608–630 BF16 TFLOP/s/chip) running at roughly 2× the throughput of unaligned GPT-OSS-120B (330 TFLOP/s/chip). For the 6-signal XProf (profiler=xplane) triage checklist, roofline and HBM budgeting rules, wall-clock ablation methodology, published Ironwood reference targets, and ongoing updates as new TPU generations and kernels land, explore the living diagnostics guide and companion patterns in the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository.

#10456. Google retiring Nest app and Google Assistant on speakers, displays in 2027

Following the Android deprecation in September, Google Assistant is going away on speakers and smart displays next year. Additionally, Google Home is fully replacing the Nest app in January.

The Nest app on Android, iOS, and the web will stop working on January 25, 2027. The Google Home app, which requires a Google Account, will be needed to “continue controlling and receiving notifications from your Nest devices remotely.”

Officially, Google wants to “focus all of [its] engineering, security, and feature investments on one unified, modern experience.” It reiterated benefits like:

With home.nest.com going away, you have to download any custom video clips since they will not transfer over to Google Home. A one-click “Download All” button is available.

Terrible news. For all my first-gen Nest cameras (wired hello doorbell, wired outdoor cams, and multiple indoor cams), I vastly prefer the Nest app to the terrible Google Home app.

Otherwise, Nest app users do not need to do anything else, with devices automatically transferring over to the Google Home app. No Nest thermostats, cameras, doorbells, smoke alarms, or locks are losing support with this change.

Additionally, Nest Aware (1st Gen) subscriptions will automatically switch to Google Home Premium “with no additional cost and no credit card re-entry required at this time.”

The other change announced today is that Google Assistant on speakers and smart displays is going away on August 2, 2027. It will be replaced by the Gemini for Home voice assistant. Google notes that natural language support replaces “rigid, static and highly specific voice commands.” Additionally:

You can upgrade to Gemini today, while Google is expanding international availability over the coming months. Google will share more updates closer to the deadline.

#10447. 5 Things Most Developers Get Wrong When Turning Images into HTML

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Turning a design image into a working webpage can look simple.

You have a picture (a mockup, a screenshot, a Figma export), and your job is to turn it into working HTML in a browser.

But here is the catch. A design image is static, while a webpage is dynamic and responsive across different screen sizes and devices. A webpage needs to load quickly, adapt perfectly to different screen sizes, and allow touch interactions on mobile devices.

Most mistakes in this process happen when the conversion is treated as copying a picture instead of rebuilding it.

This section walks you through the mistakes developers often make, and more importantly, how to fix each of them.

Whether you are slicing a client mockup or converting a screenshot into code, avoiding these mistakes can save you from hours of rework. Let’s get into the details.

A screenshot reflects how a page appears at a particular display. But it doesn’t show how the layout will behave when the content changes or the screen gets smaller.

Developers sometimes try to copy the exact positions and dimensions of every element. To match the screenshot, they may use fixed widths, large margins, and absolute positioning.

This can work at one screen size, but the layout may break on smaller screens or when a heading wraps onto an extra line.

The better approach is identifying the layout rules behind the design. CSS Grid can arrange the cards in columns. Flexbox can help align elements within each card. And media queries can adjust the layout for smaller screens.

Pixel-level comparison can be useful in some cases, but it should not come at the cost of a flexible layout.

When developers recreate a design, it’s easier for them to focus on shapes and spacing rather than the purpose of each element. So, they may use generic elements for headings, buttons, navigation areas, and content sections instead of choosing elements that reflect each element's purpose.

This may produce the right appearance, but it often makes the code harder to maintain and understand. And when developers fail to use the right HTML elements, it can also create accessibility problems later.

So don’t put everything inside several unnamed containers. Instead, use a clear structure with appropriate headings, meaningful alt text for informative images, and real buttons for actions.

Semantic elements (e.g., header, nav, main, section, and footer) can also help organize a large page when they match the content’s purpose.

A design image may have headings, descriptions, prices, labels, or button text.

Reusing the entire image to reproduce the original design feels quick. But it can create problems when that text has to work as part of a responsive web page.

For example, there’s a promotional banner that contains a heading, a short description, and a CTA button. If you use the entire banner as one image, you can’t easily change the heading, translate the text, or make the button work as a real element.

So, don’t treat the image like that. Instead, use the original image where appropriate and recreate the text and button in HTML. CSS can control their position, size, color, and spacing.

Make sure you distinguish between visual elements that should remain images and text that should be recreated as HTML.

Not every part of turning images into HTML needs to be done manually.

Developers sometimes spend too much time on recreating basic elements when there are free online conversion tools available. These tools can help them get an initial version much faster.

An online conversion tool may be helpful when you need to extract text or generate a rough starting layout from a design image.

For example, you can upload a design image into an online image to html converter to get a starting point (rather than starting from a blank file). Review the tool’s output. Identify the parts you can reuse (as they are) and tweak the parts that need adjustments.

Keep in mind that the first result generated by the tool isn’t the final code. You may need to reorganize the HTML, refine the CSS, and adjust the layout to make it responsive.

Use automation where it saves time and your own judgement where it matters.

A screenshot shows just one view of a page. It doesn’t show how the layout may change on a phone or how people using a keyboard/screen reader can interact with it.

So, developers who focus only on the desktop design may end up with:

content that becomes too small on mobile devices

There’s also a chance of accessibility problems when images lack suitable alt text/interactive elements.

Let’s say a page has three cards in a row. On the desktop, this arrangement may work well. But on a phone, you may need to stack the cards vertically so their content remains readable.

That’s why accessibility should be considered during the same process. Use meaningful headings and provide appropriate alt text for images. Also, maintain sufficient text contrast and make sure interactive elements can be operated with a keyboard.

Lastly, don’t forget to test the page on both desktop and mobile screen sizes before finalizing it.

HTML and CSS handle the structure and appearance of a webpage, but JavaScript makes it possible to add interactive features.

For example, a design image may contain a navigation menu with a button. When recreating this design, developers should make sure the button performs an actual action rather than simply looking like a clickable element.

You can use JavaScript's event listeners to handle these interactions.

Now, add JavaScript to make the menu functional:

In this example, clicking the button opens or closes the navigation menu. The aria-expanded attribute also communicates the menu's current state to assistive technologies.

This is a simple example of why converting a design image into HTML involves more than matching its appearance. Developers also need to recreate the behavior of interactive elements using appropriate HTML, CSS, and JavaScript.

The process of turning an image into HTML feels easy when you treat it the right way.

Start by identifying the main sections, text, images, and interactive elements. Then create meaningful HTML and build the layout with CSS. Make the necessary adjustments to ensure it works across different screen sizes.

Use a conversion tool when it can really save time. But don’t forget to review the generated output of the tool thoroughly before using it.

A screenshot is a visual reference, not a complete set of development instructions. And the best result for you isn’t simply a page that looks like the image.

It is a page that also works well, adapts well, and stays easy to maintain.

#10434. Retail Merchandising Principles for Web Designers and Developers: Display Design, Ranking Rules and Measurement

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every e-commerce grid, product page and checkout screen is a shelf. Merchandisers have spent decades working out what to put at eye level, how many choices to show and how to tell whether a display works, and web teams are now rebuilding the same logic in code: sort orders, pinned products, badges, filters and A/B tests. Knowing where those ideas came from helps you build them with fewer mistakes.

This article maps physical retail merchandising onto digital product design. It covers visual hierarchy and choice load in listing UIs, a small rule-based ranking function you can test, accessible price and badge design with a contrast checker, and a significance test for layout experiments. It also covers why good plans fail at the handoff between design, engineering and operations. The numbers in the examples are illustrative, so use your own data. The code was run with Node.js 22.

Merchandising shapes how products are displayed, promoted and made accessible across sales channels. A well-planned strategy considers visual hierarchy, packaging, promotional materials, inventory timing and consistency between locations. For example, a skincare product may struggle to convert if testers are missing, shelf information is unclear or displays arrive after a campaign begins. If you build for the web, nearly every one of these problems has a direct counterpart:

Customers scan before they read. Nielsen Norman Group defines visual hierarchy as the arrangement of elements so the eye moves through them in order of importance, and names color and contrast, scale and grouping as the main tools. On a shelf, a larger facing or a contrasting background makes the hero product visible first. In a product grid, the equivalents are a larger card for a featured item, a clear boundary around a bundle, and a restrained use of badges so that only one or two items carry emphasis.

A common mistake is giving every card a badge, a color and a call to action. When everything is emphasized, nothing is, and the page reads like a crowded table in a store. Treat emphasis as a limited resource and have the design system say how many emphasized cards one screen may show.

A premium item placed beside entry-level alternatives may lose perceived value, and a crowded display can make fresh products look less appealing. Hick’s Law states that the time to make a decision increases with the number and complexity of choices, and its practical advice is to reduce options when speed matters and to highlight recommended ones. In a listing UI that suggests a curated first screen with a clear sort label, a few high-confidence recommendations and filters that handle the long tail, rather than an unbroken wall of products. Hick’s Law is a guide, not a rule, so treat it as a hypothesis to test on your own audience.

Merchandisers often ask for things like “pin the new range to the top, boost this collection, never show more than two items from one brand in a row, and push sold-out items down”. Implemented as scattered CSS order values and one-off queries, these requests become hard to audit. A small, pure function keeps the rules explicit and testable.

Because the function has no side effects, a short test can check every rule. This one confirms that pinned items lead, a pinned item that is out of stock is skipped, the brand cap pushes a third item from one brand behind other brands, sold-out items go last and a boost can lift a weaker product:

The design decision here is that stock demotion and the brand cap are defaults, while pins and boosts are inputs a merchandiser can change without a deploy, for example from a config file or admin screen. A real system would also log which rule moved each item, so that someone can answer why a product appears where it does.

Price cards and shelf labels are text, and text needs contrast. The WCAG contrast requirement asks for at least 4.5:1 for normal text and 3:1 for large text. Product cards are a place where this slips, because grey secondary prices and pastel sale badges look refined in a mockup. The following script computes the WCAG contrast ratio, so you can run it over your design tokens in CI.

It prints 4.54 for mid grey #767676 on white, which just passes for normal text, 7.00 for #595959, which would also meet the stricter enhanced level, and 3.96 for the pink #d94f70, which fails for small badge text but would pass for large text. A sale badge that fails the check should be fixed in the token, not on one page.

A useful display respects the realities of the sales environment: floor space, customer movement, staff workload, replenishment access and how much information shoppers can absorb at a glance. The digital version is the gap between the design file and what ships. Test the setup with the people who will use it, not only with the team that created it, because their feedback reveals practical problems before a wide rollout.

A polished plan can fail in practice when materials arrive late, displays are assembled incorrectly or teams miss replenishment. On the web, the matching failures are a promotion that goes live before its product data is synced, a badge that stays on after a sale ends, and a hero banner that links to a discontinued item. Execution needs coordination across marketing, suppliers, logistics and engineering, with clear instructions, realistic schedules, display standards and checks. Some companies outsource this kind of field merchandising. One provider’s services page, for example, lists planogram and retail resets, end-cap displays, signage placement, on-shelf availability, in-store audits and visit reporting. Whether or not you hire help, that list is a useful checklist of what execution involves, and most items have a digital twin you can automate: availability checks, expiry dates on promotions and audit reports. Treat any performance figures on vendor pages as the vendor’s own claims and ask for evidence before relying on them.

Timing matters most for seasonal promotions and limited windows. A campaign that goes live after peak demand has passed cannot deliver the same value, so give promotional content a start and end date in the data model and have a scheduled job remove it, rather than relying on someone to remember.

Sales alone cannot tell you whether visibility, placement, stockouts, confusing information or poor setup caused a result. For a web listing, the answer is a controlled experiment. The script below compares conversion for a control grid and a variant with a two-proportion z-test, a standard way to check whether a difference is larger than chance would explain.

With these illustrative numbers, conversion rises from 3.00 percent to 3.90 percent, a relative lift of 30.0 percent, and the z value of 2.21 exceeds the 1.96 threshold for roughly 95 percent confidence. That does not prove the curated grid is better in general. It says this difference is unlikely to be noise for this test, and it assumes you fixed the sample size in advance and did not stop the moment the result looked good. Use a proper experimentation platform or a statistician for anything with real revenue at stake.

A strong merchandising operation does not require a large budget. A simple guide for product priorities, layout, pricing, replenishment and approved materials keeps execution consistent, and it is the physical version of a design system: a small set of approved components and rules that make the correct result the easy path. For a product card, that means a documented component with a fixed set of badge types, a defined maximum of emphasized items per screen and tokens that already pass the contrast check.

Consistency also protects trust. Damaged fixtures, inconsistent packaging, missing prices and outdated signs make good products look poorly managed, and the digital equivalents are broken images, stale prices and mismatched labels between the listing and the product page. Aim for consistency, not identical pages: a mobile grid and a kiosk screen can differ, but they should share product priorities, visual identity and core messages.

Temporary and in-store selling spaces demand quick communication. Visitors move fast, attention is limited, and interfaces must be clear from a distance and forgiving of mistakes. If you build kiosk, tablet or point-of-sale software, these constraints shape the interface:

When results disappoint, audit the real experience before commissioning a redesign. Think like a first-time customer: is the offer clear, are premium items distinct and can the team maintain the page easily? Government small-business guidance offers concrete analogues. The Queensland Government’s retail display guidance recommends showing essential information and prices so customers can decide quickly, placing popular or profitable items at eye level, and refreshing window displays every two to four weeks. The Westminster City Council guide similarly links visual merchandising, layout and planned promotions to attracting customers and encouraging them to spend more time and money in a business.

Prioritize visibility, clarity, stock accuracy and consistent execution before investing in a full redesign. Small operational improvements can make a meaningful difference, especially when they remove obstacles that repeatedly interrupt the buying process.

The public guidance cited here was written for general small-business use, and UX principles such as visual hierarchy and Hick’s Law come from interface research, so they are useful analogies rather than guarantees. The code is a teaching sketch: it ignores personalization, pagination, caching, search relevance and many other concerns of a production ranking system. Results vary with category, audience and device, so test changes in your own setting and treat any benchmark as a starting point.

What is merchandising in e-commerce? It is how products are displayed, ordered, promoted and made accessible across a site or app, including layout, labels, badges, sort rules and inventory handling.

How does visual hierarchy apply to a product grid? The same tools apply: contrast, scale and grouping decide what the eye sees first and which products read as related.

What should I measure first? Start with conversion, add-to-cart rate and stock availability for the listing, then add average order value and revenue per visitor once the basics are tracked.

How do I know a layout test is reliable? Fix the sample size in advance, randomize users, run it long enough to cover weekly patterns and check significance, for example with the two-proportion test above.

Do small teams need a big design system? No. A short component guide with a few badge types, spacing rules and accessible color tokens is enough to keep pages consistent.

Merchandising is applied interface design with a long history. Clear hierarchy, a curated first screen, readable prices, explicit ranking rules and controlled experiments carry over directly from the shelf to the screen. For designers and developers, the practical lesson is that a good layout only counts if it ships correctly, stays current and is measured. Audit the basics, put the rules in code, test one change at a time and record what you learn.

#10423. 5 AI Tools for Creating Audio-Led Music Videos: A Practical Workflow

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You can open on the singer’s first line, cut to another image as the instruments answer, and return to the singer for the key line of the chorus. If that return depends on a convincing close-up, test it before building the rest of the video. Choose a phrase with a quick consonant followed by a held vowel. Those two sounds give you clear points to check against the recorded vocal.

Use the recorded vocal as the timing reference. AI tools can help build opening shots, instrumental cutaways, and singing performances. Compare MakeSong, HeyGen, and Hedra on the singing shots themselves, and try Freebeat for an audio-led visual draft.

For creators developing multimedia experiences, the workflow also connects with broader web technologies. SitePoint's guide to building better video experiences on the web explores some of the practical considerations involved in working with video content.

AISong provides an image-led way to build the visual world around a recorded track. It fits a performance video that uses atmosphere and supporting shots in addition to views of the singer.

AISong’s AI Music Video Generator combines the song with a set of image references for a multi-shot sequence. Use that feature for the opening, an instrumental cutaway, or an establishing scene that can support the performance footage. Check whether the setting and the singer’s close-up look like they belong in the same video.

When preparing these assets for a website or multimedia project, consistency between images and video clips is important. This is also relevant to the process of building an AI media pipeline from concept art to web video.

Portrait art, rehearsal stills, or images of the setting can give these supporting shots an established look. It is particularly useful when some parts of the song call for visuals that give the audience a break from the performer.

MakeSong can build a video around the recorded track and reference images. Its lip-sync option requires a real human face. Start with a clear frontal image and check the result against the original vocal.

MakeSong’s AI Music Video Generator accepts audio, images, and a written prompt. The product page also offers lip sync for eligible face references. Give the close-up a particular vocal phrase and reserve a wider shot for the next musical response. Check mouth timing and the performer’s appearance at the cut.

This approach works when a single visual brief needs to connect the performance with the supporting scenes. The artist should have an approved reference and a clear idea of when the singer should appear.

HeyGen is a performer-led music video option. It is particularly relevant when the same on-screen singer should remain recognizable through more than one setting or camera distance.

Its music video workflow accepts a song and supports singing performance. Use a close and a wider view to compare mouth timing and continuity on the same vocal phrase. Watch for changes in the face or outfit when the framing shifts.

Choose this route when viewers need to follow the same singer across several scenes. This makes more sense than a single avatar clip when the performer is the visual thread for the whole video.

Hedra can animate a portrait from an audio clip. Try a single line with a quick consonant followed by a held vowel to see where the mouth falls behind the recording.

A clear portrait and one clean line give you a controlled test. Inspect the start and end of the phrase, then check how the clip cuts to the next shot. When only one line is convincing, use that line and let the original edit carry the rest.

A short close-up or a fictional singer’s entrance in the chorus is a good use for this approach. It is a practical fit when you already have an edit and only need one additional vocal moment.

Freebeat offers an audio-to-video workflow with a performance-oriented mode. It gives artists another way to test a performance cut driven by an uploaded track.

Use its singing video route for an excerpt with an audible pause and a clear vocal re-entry. Compare whether the performance feels natural during the pause and whether the visual action resumes where the vocal does. Keep the same approved track for the comparison.

Try this early to compare a full performance treatment with a version built from shorter clips before settling on an edit.

Decide how much of the release the singer needs to carry. An image-led sequence can support the track around key lines, a performer-led workflow can keep the singer present through the full edit, and an avatar clip can cover a single important phrase.

For developers and designers working with audio and video on the web, the final production stage also deserves attention. Video formats, playback behavior, and audio synchronization can affect how the finished content performs across devices. SitePoint's tutorial on the Web Audio API provides useful background on handling audio in web applications.

Judge the hardest line first and use the original vocal as the reference at every cut. Once the performance works, build the supporting shots around it and prepare the final video for its intended platform.

#10420. Agents are sessions, not requests: size GKE node pools for AI agent sessions

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Your company wants to move its coding agents from developer laptops to a shared GKE cluster, for persistence, scale, and governance. Each of those agent sessions stays open all day but works only part of it, and how you run them decides how much of your node bill pays for waiting.

By the end of this article, you will know why an agent session needs a different runtime than a web request, how Agent Substrate and AX run sessions on GKE, and how to identify cost optimization opportunities in your GKE node pools. This article assumes you know Kubernetes Pods and node pools. If not, check out the GKE overview.

A web request lasts milliseconds. The server answers it, keeps nothing, and serves the next one, so Kubernetes scales a web app by its traffic: more requests, more Pods.

An agent session works another way. A coding agent's workspace can stay open for the developer's whole workday: it keeps its memory and its files between prompts, and it works only after a prompt arrives.

How much of that day does the agent really work? Much less than it waits. In a July 2026 study of agent traces, Banruo Liu and colleagues found that in a typical session with several prompts, the agent waits for the user about 80% of the time. A turn, one prompt and the agent's whole response to it, takes about 6.5 minutes of agent work on average

Put those numbers on one developer's day. With 2 prompts an hour over an 8 hour workday, which is our assumption, the agent works 22% of the day and waits for nearly 78% of it. If each session holds a machine, you pay for that waiting time

Teams usually pick one of two answers, and both work well for web apps.

The first is to give each session its own Pod. The session keeps its state, but its Pod holds 1 vCPU and 1 GiB all day. For example, a c3-standard-4 node fits 3 such Pods after GKE's reservations, so in our cost model 100 coding agents need 34 nodes and about USD 1,288 a month, for agents that work 22% of the time.

The second is to scale the Pod to zero between prompts. The node's capacity is released, and files on a persistent volume survive, but the process and its memory do not, so the next prompt starts a new agent process.

Based on that, you want a third option: keep the session, and free the machine while the session waits.

Agent Substrate and AX are built for that: the developer's session stays open, and the sandbox that serves it gives its machine back while the session waits. Together they cover the parts of an agentic platform that are the same for most teams: the control plane, the runtime, and the sandbox. Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. AX answers what the agent is.

Following the CNCF donation of gVisor, Agent Substrate, and OpenRL in November 2026, you can run Agent Substrate on GKE or on other Kubernetes clusters. Agent Substrate 1.0 is generally available on GKE.

One distinction helps from here on. The session is the multi turn interaction between the developer and the agent, with its conversation history. The compute that serves it is an isolated sandbox: an actor in Agent Substrate, or a Task in AX. Parking acts on the sandbox, and the session stays open.

Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. It separates the stateful agent from the compute it runs on. An actor is the sandbox for one agent, and it holds the agent's running processes, files, and identity. A worker is a reusable sandbox drawn from a warm pool of Kubernetes Pods, and one worker hosts several actors.

An agent runs code that a model wrote, so anything in its environment can leak through a prompt injection or a bad command, API keys included. Gateways control the actor's inbound and outbound traffic and add credentials on the way out, based on the actor's identity, so credentials stay out of the agent's environment.

SuspendActor snapshots an idle actor to object storage and frees its worker, and the next request restores the actor on a warm worker with room for it. Agent Substrate snapshots memory by default and falls back to files when it cannot capture memory. Many parked sessions share a small warm pool, so in our cost model the same 100 agents need 16 nodes instead of 34.

AX answers what the agent is and what it knows. You declare a Task, which says what runs (image, command, and limits), and a Workspace, which says what the agent knows (repositories and files). AX runs each Task as an actor. ax suspend parks the task and keeps its /workspace. Today AX asks for a volume snapshot, so the files come back on resume and the agent's processes start fresh (runner guide).

AX passes suspend, resume, and delete to Agent Substrate. The AX roadmap plans to add budgets, policies, and gateways, which limit what an agent may do, and a record of what each task did.

Two details decide the bill before you size anything.

First, you decide when a sandbox parks. Agent Substrate does not suspend an idle actor by itself (#424), so your agent harness, or the service in front of it, calls the suspend API after an idle timeout. Until then, an idle actor keeps its worker. When idle actors hold every worker, a request for another actor waits up to 5 seconds by default, then fails with HTTP 503 (Agent Substrate docs).

Second, you decide how much CPU each sandbox is promised. An actor is sized by its CPU and memory limits, and each worker books those limits against the capacity you declare for its pool (API guide). Our cost model sets that capacity to what the node really has, so every actor keeps its full limit. You can declare more capacity than the node has, and then you overcommit CPU at the pool instead of in each Pod.

Our cost model prices parking for the same 100 coding agents, at Google Cloud on-demand list prices, as of November 2026, in us-central1.

With a 2 minute idle timeout, each session holds a worker nearly 29% of its open time. A warm pool sized for bursts (the 99th percentile of sessions holding a worker at once, if sessions act independently, plus 15% headroom) needs 15 nodes. Add one node that runs around the clock for the Agent Substrate control plane and its PostgreSQL database, and a job that scales the pool to zero after hours:

Parking saves nearly 40% of the node bill. The pool is full about once in 3,000 turns, so a prompt rarely meets that 503. In an hour at 2.5 prompts instead of 2, the same 15 nodes are full for 3% of turns, so size the pool for your busiest hour.

Keep in mind that parking pays most when your agents wait the most. At 2 prompts an hour, the saving is nearly 40%. At 4 prompts an hour, the agent works 44% of the hour, the pool grows to 27 nodes, and the saving is 4%. Above about 4 prompts an hour, one Pod per session becomes the cheaper option, so measure your prompt rate first.

Also, the pool size is yours to set. The autoscaling option in the GKE install guide follows worker Pods, not suspended actors, so scale the pool to zero after hours with a scheduled job: a pool that runs all 730 hours of the month costs nearly twice the one Pod per session bill.

Finally, the fleet size matters. The Agent Substrate control plane costs the same for 10 sessions or 1,000, so parking pays from about 37 coding sessions, or 13 for an assumed chat assistant that works about a minute an hour.

Overcommit changes the comparison. If your Pods request less CPU than their limit, Kubernetes places them by their requests. A Pod that requests 0.25 vCPU with a 1 vCPU limit packs 12 to a node (its 1 GiB of memory caps it there), so the same 100 coding agents fit on 9 nodes, fewer than our parked pool. Agent Substrate can overcommit too, through the capacity you declare on the worker pool, and our cost model does not price that case.

Overcommit has the same cost on both sides: agents share CPU and slow down when their neighbors are busy, during builds and tests. Compare like with like before you decide to park: Pods and actors with the same CPU guarantee.

Back to the shared cluster. Agents are sessions, not requests: size the warm pool for the sessions that hold a worker, not the sessions that are open.

We would park long lived sessions that wait most of the hour, and keep one Pod per session when developers prompt more than about 4 times an hour. Size the warm pool for working time plus a short idle timeout at your busiest hour, and compare with your current Pods at the same CPU guarantee before you park.

Thanks for reading! If you run agent sessions on Kubernetes today, how many prompts an hour do your users send? The prompt rate is the input we are least sure of. You can reach Ivan on LinkedIn or X with your numbers, questions, and corrections.

#10409. Sizing GPU Memory for a RAG Service: KV Cache, Context and Concurrency

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) app is usually drawn as a language model plus a vector database. When it goes live, the part that fails first is rarely the retrieval. It is GPU memory on the generation model, because a model that loads fine for one request can run out of memory when several users send long questions at once.

This article shows how to estimate that limit before you deploy, with a short script you can run, and then covers what else in the pipeline can slow responses down.

Serving a model takes more than its weights. The GPU also holds:

the key-value (KV) cache, which grows with the length of every active request

memory used by the inference runtime itself

SitePoint's guide to running a local LLM on 10GB of VRAM makes the same point for a single desktop GPU: the usable budget is the total VRAM minus overhead, and the KV cache grows with the context window, so the real model budget is smaller than the number on the box.

For a RAG service, the KV cache matters even more. Retrieved passages are pasted into the prompt, so every request carries a long context, and every concurrent request holds its own cache.

The cache size per token depends on the model's architecture: two tensors (keys and values) for every layer, for every KV head. This script turns those numbers into a rough limit on concurrent requests:

def kv_bytes_per_token(layers, kv_heads, head_dim, dtype_bytes=2):

# Keys and values, for every layer, for one token.

return 2 *layers *kv_heads *head_dim *dtype_bytes

def max_concurrent(vram_gb, weights_gb, ctx_tokens, per_token, reserve_gb=2.0):

budget = (vram_gb - weights_gb - reserve_gb) * GIB

return int(budget // (ctx_tokens * per_token))

per_token = kv_bytes_per_token(32, 8, 128)

print(per_token // 1024, "KiB per token")

n = max_concurrent(24, 16, ctx, per_token)

print(ctx, "tokens ->", n, "concurrent requests")

For a 24 GB GPU holding about 16 GB of fp16 weights with 2 GB kept in reserve, it prints:

The numbers are estimates. Real runtimes add their own overhead, and the architecture values come from the model's configuration file, so read them from the model you actually use. The pattern is what matters: doubling the context length halves the number of requests that fit. A RAG prompt that stuffs in ten retrieved chunks costs real capacity.

Serving runtimes do not simply crash when the cache is full. The vLLM documentation on optimization and tuning explains that when KV cache space is insufficient for all batched requests, the engine preempts some of them and recomputes them later, which can hurt end-to-end latency. Its suggested remedies include giving the cache more memory, reducing the number of concurrent sequences or batched tokens, or sharding the model across GPUs.

So the symptom of undersizing is often not an error. It is slow, uneven responses under load. Test with realistic prompt lengths and several simultaneous users, not a single request.

Embedding and ingestion have a different profile from generation. Embedding is often lighter than serving a large language model, and some teams run it on CPU. Document parsing, chunking and tokenization also run on CPU, so a fast GPU cannot make up for a slow CPU upstream. SitePoint's tutorial on a local RAG system for private documents walks through chunking, embeddings, vector storage and reranking, and is a good map of which stages run where.

RAM: vector indexes, document loaders and caches all live in system memory, so large collections need more of it.

Storage: model files, indexes, logs and backups add up. Fast storage shortens model loading and re-indexing.

Network: if the vector database, application server and model server sit in different places, latency adds to every request.

Two layouts are common. A single instance runs everything and is simplest for prototypes and internal tools. Separated services put ingestion and retrieval on smaller machines and keep the GPU for generation, which scales better as traffic grows.

Memory estimates only hold if the GPU memory is really yours. On a shared or time-sliced GPU, other workloads can take memory and compute away from your service, which makes capacity hard to plan. Hosting providers describe this in different terms, such as passthrough, vGPU, MIG and time-slicing. For a view of how one GPU hosting provider explains those allocation methods, GPU Mart's GPU virtualization guide compares them. It is a hosting company's page, so confirm any provider's claims about isolation and performance with your own load test.

Whatever provider you choose, ask these questions:

Is the physical GPU assigned to you exclusively, or partitioned or time-sliced?

Do you have root access to install your own drivers, CUDA libraries and containers?

Which minimum commitments, regions and support terms apply?

A small stack can run as a handful of containers: an API service for request handling and prompt assembly, an ingestion worker, a vector database and a model server. Keeping them separate lets you monitor each one's memory and CPU use and move the heavy one to bigger hardware without rewriting the rest.

For a service that stays up all month, a fixed monthly term is easier to forecast than hourly capacity. Hourly capacity suits testing quantization settings or processing a one-off document batch.

Is GPU memory always the bottleneck for RAG?

Not always. Generation usually hits memory first, but slow parsing, a small CPU or a distant vector database can dominate response time.

Does quantization solve the memory problem?

It shrinks the weights, but the KV cache still grows with context length and concurrency. Test with your real prompt sizes.

It depends on volume. Small workloads can run on CPU, while a large ingestion job may benefit from a GPU for a limited time.

Size a RAG service for the worst realistic moment, not the single-request demo. Estimate the KV cache from the model's architecture, remember that long retrieved contexts reduce how many requests fit, watch for preemption under load, keep CPU, RAM, storage and network in the plan, and find out whether the GPU is exclusive or shared. The script above is small, but it turns a vague worry about memory into numbers you can check.

#10402. What’s !important #19: CSS Refraction Effects, Scroll State Queries, @custom-media, and More

We’re back! Don’t worry, this isn’t a trick (despite what our name suggests!). However, due to the hiatus, we’re also a little behind. That’s why this issue of What’s !important is absolutely stacked. All killer, no filler. Here’s what you missed.

By the way, before we dive in, Mat Marquis’ highly recommended JavaScript for Everyone course is currently -30% off (code: CSSTRICKSFRIENDS). You don’t want to miss this.

Preethi Sam demonstrated how to create background image refraction effects with CSS. The effects are stunning, can be animated, don’t need very much CSS (no JavaScript), and we don’t need to duplicate the image either.

Brecht De Ruyte demonstrated how to build customizable components using container style queries and advanced attr(), showcasing some incredible interactive demos, including the two below:

Dan Holloran showed us how to use the different scroll-state() queries (scrollable, scrolled, snapped, and stuck). It’s not a long read, which is exactly what you want for a new but simple feature like this.

A quick, no-context CSS snippet to entice you:

Declan Chidlow shared some of the craziest CSS to ever exist, and I mean craziest. Most of it comes from Internet Explorer, but let’s face it, you knew that already. Thankfully, they don’t build them like they used to.

Bramus demonstrated how to animate CSS grids using anchor positioning. The idea is that we anchor content to its corresponding cell, and then as the grid reflows, it’s the content (not the cell) that animates with it.

(But it doesn’t work in Firefox due to what sounds like a bug.)

Chris Coyier talked about a bunch of new HTML features, and honestly, there’s a whole lot in there that you definitely haven’t heard of. Like, I’m certain of it.

Victor Ayomipo contemplated why container query adoption has been so terrible despite them topping CSS wishlists for years. Victor thinks that we’re using them wrong, so he’s here to set the record straight.

Cory LaViska demonstrated how to use @custom-media in addition to container style queries as a fallback.

Also, don’t forget that Declan has an extremely thorough @custom-media deep-dive in our Almanac.

Ahmad Shadeed tackled an interesting topic — detecting overlapping elements (with CSS, of course). If you’re keen on anchor positioning, scroll-driven animations, and again (!), container style queries, then this walkthrough is for you.

I want to know when the decorative element hits the section from the left edge, taking into consideration that the element might have a fluid position or size that changes with the viewport size or container size. Basically, it’s a dynamic problem. It’s not about using a media query to hide the decorative element, I want to hide it upon overlapping.

Maybe container queries are finally picking up?

Patrick Brosset walked us through a proposal for the previewsrc HTML attribute, which would enable us to supply preview images for images that haven’t yet loaded using only HTML. For example, we could supply a blurry preview image. However, I’d rather have something like :loaded/:not(:loaded) so that we can style/transition unloaded images with CSS rather than needing to create extra images.

Temani Afif shared a custom borders demo, where the trick is to declare a transparent border, use border-shape to define its shape, then declare a background in addition to background-clip: border-area so that it only shows up within the border, like this (requires Chrome at the moment):

In a similar manner, Leon Arnott shared this awesome trick for animating the background color of ::selection, something that’s actually forbidden (or so we thought, anyway…):

Today in CSS: We all know you can change the highlight colour of selected text, but it's impossible to animate that colour. Unless… the colour was just a variable inherited from the parent element, using @​property to make it animatable… Hahahahaha… [image or embed]

And that’s it, really. Simply use var(--bg-color) on ::selection or some other visually restrictive element.

Chris Coyier demonstrated how to random()ly animate an element along the edge of another element. There’s a cute doggo, by the way. Probably should’ve led with that.

Polypane explained how we might disable the root scroller accidentally (which is surprisingly easy to do — I’ve definitely done it), what we lose in doing so, and what to do instead.

Also, have you ever completely misunderstood how the root (:root/html) works? This is a solid explainer for that, too.

Speaking of accidents, have you ever tapped on a target twice, apparently in quick succession, and ended up zooming in? Andy Bell revealed that touch-action: manipulation disables the double-tap-to-zoom-in functionality. It isn’t new, but I don’t see many web developers using it. One for the CSS reset, I think?

Chrome and Firefox now ship every two weeks. The inaugural releases were fairly exciting (not smaller, as I assumed they’d be). In addition, Safari 27 (along with iOS, iPadOS, and macOS 27) shipped with some of the same features as Firefox, making them Baseline in the same release window. High fives all around!

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Pre-order it today!

For the CSS grid animation with anchor positioning, there is indeed a Firefox bug, tracked at https://bugzilla.mozilla.org/show_bug.cgi?id=1924226

#10401. Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

As highlighted in our announcement post, we said that each new model generation lets agents do more on their own. However, the more an agent can reach, the more one bad decision can cost. You've probably seen a few of these in the news lately:

Because these events produced no system crashes or logging errors, the teams behind these agents had no way to know until the damage occurred.

Agent Anomaly Detection on the Gemini Platform watches for exactly these issues. It reads the traces and logs your agents already produce and flags sessions where an agent goes outside the job it was built for.

Before you point it at your production agents, you'll probably want to see it work in a test environment. In this hands-on guide, we’ll walk you step-by-step through setting up Agent Anomaly Detection and using it to capture your very first agent behavior anomaly. By the end of this post, you'll have:

To get started, open the companion notebook Detect anomalous agent behavior with Agent Anomaly Detection in a separate tab (available on Google Colab or GitHub). As you read through this guide, it will prompt you when to execute notebook cells or interact directly with the Google Cloud console.

If a step can be done in the UI console, this post shows that path, with screenshots. The notebook does the same thing through the Agent Anomaly Detection API, so by the end you'll also have the code to automate enablement and pull findings into your own tooling.

2. A place to run the notebook: Google Colab, Colab Enterprise, or a local Jupyter environment. The notebook installs the latest ADK for Python itself. Agent Anomaly Detection is compatible with agents running ADK 1.2+, though ADK 2.0+ is preferred for best results.

3. A Cloud Logging bucket and an Observability dataset in either US or EU multi-region. (Both resources need to be in the same multi-region)

4. In the notebook: run the Get started cells. They install the packages, authenticate you, take your project ID, and turn on the four APIs that Agent Anomaly Detection needs.

First, let’s create a Customer Support Agent called “anomaly-detection-refund-demo”. Our example agent is responsible for helping customers check their order history and request refunds. However, it has a deliberate flaw: the agent has access to an overprivileged fetch_records tool which can read any table, including a `payroll` table containing sensitive employee data. All three parts of the lethal trifecta are here: the agent can read private data, it takes input from people it can't vet, and it sends what it finds back in its reply. Tool permissions and guardrails should stop most of this, but they only block what someone thought to write down. Agent Anomaly Detection flags the sessions that get past them, usually within a few minutes.

The notebook has the full agent. The part that matters is the tool, which never checks the table name against an allowlist:

All data is canned and in memory, so nothing real is read or refunded. This tool is intended to be over-permissive to illustrate AAD functionality. Don't copy this tool into production.

The Step 1 cells deploy the agent to Agent Runtime. Two details in the deploy cell are what make the agent visible to Agent Anomaly Detection. First, it sets three environment variables on the deployment, which turn on telemetry and write prompt and response content to Cloud Logging, where detection looks for it:

Second, it sends the agent one test message right after deploying. Agent Anomaly Detection discovers agents through their telemetry, so an agent that has never served a request does not appear under Monitored agents.

The deployment takes about 5 to 10 minutes and prints the agent's resource name and the reply to the test message:

For more information on how to create and deploy an agent, take a look at these guides: Creating an Agent and Deploying an Agent.

In the console: The sample code we provide already enables logging and telemetry when deploying the agent. Verify this by navigating to the Agent Deployments page (Gemini Platform > Agents > Deployments) and clicking “Configure” next to the recently created anomaly-detection-refund-demo agent to open the Observability Service Configuration.

Great! You now have a working agent that is successfully configured and ready to be monitored by Agent Anomaly Detection.

Now let’s set up Agent Anomaly Detection to monitor your agent’s traffic.

In the console: navigate to the Agent Platform > Agents > Audit page and click “Enable”.

Choose your agent’s Logging Bucket and Observability Dataset, making sure they are in the same region (us-multi-region or eu-multi-region) enable “Automatically monitor new agents”, then click “Agree and continue” and Agent Anomaly Detection will be set up in your project.

If you'd rather do this in code, the first Step 2 cell in the notebook creates the same configuration through the API, using the default _Default bucket and _Trace dataset with automatic monitoring on.

Setup takes around 30 minutes. Once it’s done loading you’re ready to proceed to the next step! Because you enabled automatic monitoring and the agent already served a request in Step 1, setup picks it up right away. Check that it shows as Active under Settings > Monitored agents before you continue; sessions that start earlier aren't analyzed.

Now that the agent is being monitored, let's see Agent Anomaly Detection in action! The Step 3 cells send one normal conversation and one anomalous conversation, each in its own session. The normal conversation is a customer listing their orders and getting a refund for a broken lamp. The anomalous conversation simulates a user who finds the fetch_records flaw and uses it to pull payroll data:

Notice that the user brings almost nothing: a customer ID and one first name. Asking for a table that doesn't exist makes the agent reveal the existing tables, which is how they learn there is a payroll table within reach.

The notebook prints both transcripts with the agent's tool calls, and the session IDs you'll look up next.

Anomaly Detection will scan the sessions within 5 minutes. In the landing page, you should see that a couple sessions have been processed and one anomalous session detected.

Let’s take a look at the anomalous session in more detail. Click the session id to open the detail view or paste the anomalous session id into the search bar.

In our run, the top finding was Tool misuse at High severity, with 95% probability. Your detectors and severities may differ, because the models behind the analysis are non-deterministic. The recommended solution is to restrict the `fetch_records` tool to read from specific tables.

Click an invocation in the Session pane to see its analysis in Details. Each invocation is Medium on its own, but together they make the session High.

To stay informed without watching the console, connect Agent Anomaly Detection to your existing alerting workflows.

In Cloud Monitoring: Agent Anomaly Detection publishes two metrics: Detected events, which counts anomalies by detector and severity, and Monitored agents, which tracks your detection coverage. To view these metrics, click the arrow next to "View in Cloud Monitoring" on the Anomaly detection page and select a metric to open it in Metrics Explorer.

To be notified of new anomalies, go to Monitoring > Alerting, click "Create policy", and select the Detected events metric. Leave the filters empty so the alert covers every anomaly, including the demo's, then set the rolling window to 5 minutes with the delta function, and set the threshold to above 0. Once you know what's normal for your agents, add a severity filter (for example High or Critical) so you're only alerted on the findings that matter. A metric-absence policy on Monitored agents will also alert you if monitoring stops.

In Security Command Center: Each anomaly is also published as an AGENT_SESSION_ANOMALY finding in the Security Command Center if it is enabled for your organization. Click "View in Security Command Center" to triage these findings alongside your other threats, use "Set security marks" to tag and track them, and set up notifications for high-severity findings.

You’ve now seen Agent Anomaly Detection in action: it successfully flagged the session where our agent went off-script to read payroll records, while evaluating the benign session, and correctly not flagging an anomaly. The finding told you why it was flagged and how to fix the underlying issue, and alerts in Cloud Monitoring and Security Command Center ensure you are notified of future anomalies.

When you're ready to secure your own production workloads, follow this rollout plan:

To review the complete list of prerequisites or to request Private Preview access for your project, head over to the Agent Anomaly Detection documentation.
